Efficiency is highest when punishment is more expensive, as less punishment is used. Thus, the availability and cost of punishment have a non-monotonic effect on efficiency: punishment can sustain cooperation, but its costs can outweigh its benefits for group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the cost of punishment. When punishment is cheap, it is used more, leading to higher contributions but lower efficiency due to the costs incurred by both punishers and targets. When punishment is expensive, it is used less, contributions are somewhat lower, but efficiency is higher. For prediction tasks, this means that the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness are key moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. The control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the paper's design and results allow inference about the relative efficiency across different punishment cost regimes. The findings suggest that enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the net effect depends on the balance between increased cooperation and the direct costs of punishment. Game designs with low-cost, high-effectiveness punishment are likely to see the largest increases in contribution but also the largest efficiency losses.

- source: Barclay, P. (2004). Trustworthiness and competitive altruism can also solve the tragedy of the commons. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment with Han Chinese and Mongolian Chinese students uses a standard public goods game with and without a costly punishment stage. Each group has 4 players, and each subject plays 10 rounds without punishment and 10 rounds with punishment. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR) is 0.4. Punishment costs 1 token to the punisher and reduces the punished's payoff by 3 tokens. The main findings are: (1) Punishment increases mean contribution in all treatments, but the efficiency of punishment (measured as the increase in contribution per unit of punishment) varies by group composition. (2) In all-Han and mixed-ethnicity groups, punishment is efficient (marginal effect > 1), while in all-Mongolian groups, punishment is inefficient (marginal effect < 1). (3) Ethnic diversity (mixed groups) boosts punishment efficiency for both Han and Mongolian Chinese. (4) Mongolian Chinese punish more harshly within their own group due to cultural norms ('Anda' tradition), leading to lower efficiency. (5) Han Chinese show strong ingroup bias, contributing more in all-Han groups, especially with punishment. (6) Cultural fusion (Mongolian Chinese with Han cultural background) increases contribution in mixed groups. (7) Overall, the effect of punishment on efficiency is positive in all-Han and mixed groups, but not in all-Mongolian groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence for predicting the effect of introducing punishment on efficiency in public goods games with different group compositions. For games with 4 players, 10 rounds, MPCR 0.4, and a punishment cost/impact ratio of 1:3, punishment increases efficiency in all-Han and mixed-ethnicity groups (marginal effect > 1), but is inefficient in all-Mongolian groups (marginal effect < 1). If the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, the expected efficiency with punishment will increase substantially in all-Han and mixed groups, but only modestly or not at all in all-Mongolian groups. The results are robust to a 'clear-cut' Han Chinese sample. The findings are most applicable to lab settings with similar design parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/impact, no chat, continuous contribution, no rewards, anonymous punishment, and known round count). The paper is highly informative for the prediction task, especially regarding the moderating role of group composition and cultural background.

- source: Markussen, T., Putterman, L., & Tyran, J. R. (2016). Judicial error and cooperation. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that error-free formal sanctions increase both cooperation and efficiency in a linear public goods game. When judicial errors are introduced (either type I or type II), both cooperation and efficiency decline. The effect on efficiency is more negative for type I errors than for type II errors, due to the wasteful punishment of cooperators. The negative effect of errors is stronger when the error probability is higher and when errors convert a deterrent sanction regime into a non-deterrent one. The effects of type I and type II errors on contributions are symmetric, but type I errors reduce group earnings more. Subjects show a clear preference for avoiding type I errors, but this preference is fully explained by income maximization rather than additional psychological aversion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence that the introduction of punishment with judicial error reduces efficiency in public goods games, and that the magnitude of the efficiency loss depends on the type and probability of error, as well as the deterrence strength of the sanction regime. For prediction, the findings imply that enabling punishment increases efficiency only if sanctions are deterrent and error-free; otherwise, efficiency may decrease, especially with type I errors or high error probability. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence and type of punishment, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the probability and type of judicial error, and whether sanctions are deterrent. Control efficiency (without punishment) is also reported, allowing for direct comparison.

- source: Ertan, A., Page, T., & Putterman, L. (2009). Who to punish? Individual decisions and majority rule in mitigating the free rider problem. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when groups can vote on punishment rules, they overwhelmingly prohibit punishment of high contributors and converge toward allowing punishment of low contributors only. This rule structure leads to higher contributions and, crucially, higher efficiency (group earnings as a fraction of the maximum possible) compared to both no punishment and unrestricted punishment. The cost of punishment under unrestricted rules reduces efficiency, while targeted punishment of low contributors effectively deters free riding and increases group welfare. The findings are robust across two experimental designs (3-vote and 5-vote), and the efficiency gains are statistically significant. The study provides direct evidence that the structure and targeting of punishment, not just its presence, are critical for achieving efficiency gains in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a PGG-like environment increases efficiency only if punishment is restricted to low contributors (i.e., free riders) and not allowed against high contributors. Unrestricted punishment can reduce efficiency due to costly 'perverse punishment.' The experimental design and results allow direct mapping to prediction tasks: for a 4-player, 30-round, no-chat, continuous-contribution VCM with MPCR 0.4, punishment cost 0.25 per 1 unit impact, and anonymous punishment, efficiency is highest when punishment is allowed only for low contributors. If the control (no punishment) efficiency is known, enabling targeted punishment (low-only) is likely to increase efficiency substantially; enabling unrestricted punishment may not. The findings are directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially regarding the targeting and scope of punishment.

- source: Fu, T. T., Ji, Y. N., Kamei, K., & Putterman, L. (2017). Punishment can support cooperation even when punishable. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that peer punishment increases cooperation and group earnings in a standard public goods game. When higher-order punishment (counter-punishment) is allowed, it occurs frequently, especially in response to antisocial punishment, but does not significantly reduce the positive effect of punishment on contributions or efficiency. All punishment treatments (first-order and higher-order) result in higher average earnings than the baseline with no punishment. There is no evidence that higher-order punishment deters first-order punishment or reduces efficiency. The presence of higher-order punishment does not provide a net inducement to punish non-punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with four players and 15 rounds, enabling peer punishment increases efficiency (group earnings) compared to no-punishment baseline. Adding a second or third stage for higher-order punishment (counter-punishment) does not significantly reduce efficiency or the positive effect of punishment. Thus, for similar game designs, the presence of punishment (even if punishers can be punished) is likely to increase efficiency relative to control. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Krügel, J. P., & Maaser, N. (2025). How do higher-order punishment institutions shape cooperation and norm-enforcement?. *Review of International Organizations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three conditions in a repeated linear public goods game with third-party punishment: Baseline (third party can punish), Fourth Party (third party can be sanctioned by an external observer), and Competition (two third-party candidates compete for selection by proposing punishment plans). Both Fourth Party and Competition increase average contributions and group earnings relative to Baseline. Fourth Party leads to the highest contributions and most severe/widespread punishment, but Competition achieves the highest average earnings for public good players, as punishment is more targeted and less frequent. Regression analyses confirm that both higher-order institutions significantly increase earnings and contributions, with Competition outperforming Fourth Party in terms of player payoffs. The results are robust across all periods and when focusing on later periods only.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment (and higher-order punishment institutions) on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a standard repeated linear PGG with three players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and full feedback. The Baseline (punishment-only) condition provides a control for efficiency, and the two treatments show how additional institutional features (Fourth Party monitoring, Competition) further increase efficiency. The findings support the prediction that enabling punishment increases efficiency, and that the design of the punishment institution (especially targeted, competitive punishment) can further enhance efficiency by reducing unnecessary punishment. The paper's detailed reporting of payoffs, contributions, and punishment allows for quantitative calibration of efficiency effects under different institutional designs.

- source: Botelho, A., Harrison, G. W., Pinto, L. M. C., Ross, D., & Rutström, E. E. (2022). Endogenous choice of institutional punishment mechanisms to promote social cooperation. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study replicates and extends classic public goods experiments by allowing subjects to experience both a punishment-enabled and a no-punishment institution, then vote on which to use for a high-stakes final round. Across 13 sessions, punishment increased contributions but almost always reduced average profits due to the direct costs of punishing. Only one session (out of 13) saw a majority vote for the punishment institution, and this was in a small cohort with high returns and high initial cooperation. Statistical analysis shows that subjects' votes are overwhelmingly determined by which institution gave them higher profits, not by which supported higher cooperation. The negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to variations in matching protocol, order of experience, and marginal per-capita return. The paper also reviews prior studies and finds similar patterns: punishment often fails to improve, and sometimes reduces, group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in standard public goods games with costly voluntary punishment, enabling punishment typically reduces efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline, even though it increases contributions. The effect is robust across a range of game parameters (player count, MPCR, matching protocol, order, and returns). Exceptions are rare and occur mainly when punishment is used very sparingly and cooperation is already high. For prediction tasks, the presence of costly punishment should generally be expected to lower efficiency relative to control, unless the design or context strongly favors efficient, low-use punishment. The paper also highlights that the marginal per-capita return (MPCR) and matching protocol (reputation effects) can moderate the effect, but the main driver is the costliness and frequency of punishment.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Peer punishment increases contributions in linear, nonlinear, and CPR public goods games, but does not significantly improve efficiency (payoff relative to Nash and social optimum) in any environment. In nonlinear environments, the effect of punishment on cooperation is weaker and takes longer to emerge. Punishment is less intense in nonlinear settings, likely because it is harder for subjects to identify free riders. In the CPR environment, punishment cannot fully enforce the social optimum, but can help stabilize cooperation at suboptimal levels that reduce the gains from defection. Overall, the costs of punishment erode the potential efficiency gains from increased cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in standard lab public goods and CPR games with 4 players, 10 rounds, and standard peer punishment (cost 1, impact 3, anonymous), enabling punishment increases cooperation but does not reliably increase efficiency. The effect is robust across linear and nonlinear environments, but is weaker and slower in nonlinear settings. For prediction, the presence of punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency unless the punishment mechanism is less costly or more effective than in this design. Control efficiency is a strong predictor of treatment efficiency, as punishment does not reliably improve group payoffs. Game design dimensions such as nonlinearity, complexity, and the ability to identify free riders moderate the effect of punishment on both cooperation and efficiency.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This study experimentally varies the effectiveness of punishment in a repeated linear public goods game with and without punishment. It finds that contributions increase monotonically with punishment effectiveness. However, welfare (group earnings) only improves relative to the no-punishment control when punishment effectiveness is high (at least 3 out of 4). For low effectiveness, the cost of punishment outweighs the gains from increased cooperation, resulting in lower welfare than the control. The results highlight that the design parameter of punishment effectiveness is crucial: only sufficiently strong punishment leads to both higher cooperation and higher efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games as a function of punishment effectiveness. If the control (no punishment) efficiency is known, the effect of enabling punishment depends strongly on the punishment technology: low effectiveness can reduce efficiency, while high effectiveness can increase it. The findings suggest that the marginal per-capita return, group size, and other design features held constant, the key moderator is punishment effectiveness. The results are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design, especially regarding the punishment technology parameter.

- source: Kim, H., Iris, D., Lee, J., & Tavoni, A. (2025). Representation, Peer Pressure and Punishment in a Public Goods Game. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase contributions in all treatments, but the efficiency gains depend on the decision structure. In the control (NoR, self-representation), punishment increases contributions to 75% of the endowment and does not reduce group payoffs, so efficiency is maintained or improved. In representation treatments (RnoM and RM), punishment increases contributions but also increases antisocial punishment, leading to lower group payoffs and reduced efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline. Communication (RM) between team members and representatives slightly improves outcomes compared to RnoM but does not fully restore efficiency. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is positive in self-representation but negative or neutral under representation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is moderated by the decision structure: punishment is most effective (and efficient) when individuals decide for themselves, but can reduce efficiency when representatives decide for groups, especially if communication is limited. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment will likely increase efficiency in self-representation settings, but may decrease or only modestly increase efficiency in representation settings, particularly when antisocial punishment is prevalent. The marginal per-capita return, punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness are all specified, allowing for direct mapping to prediction models. Communication channels (chat/messages) can moderate but not fully reverse negative efficiency effects under representation.

- source: Carpenter, J., Kariv, S., & Schotter, A. (2012). Network architecture, cooperation and punishment in public good experiments. *Review of Economic Design*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally studies public goods games with costly punishment under eight different network architectures, varying completeness, connectedness, and directedness. All games have four players, 15 rounds, and fixed MPCR and punishment cost. The main finding is that network structure crucially moderates the effect of punishment on both cooperation and efficiency. Complete and some connected networks achieve high efficiency because they elicit high contributions with relatively low punishment expenditures. In contrast, directed and disconnected networks often see more punishment and lower efficiency, despite sometimes higher contributions. The marginal benefit of adding monitoring edges is non-linear: adding edges to sparse networks increases efficiency, but in denser networks, more edges can actually reduce efficiency due to over-punishment. The paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency impact of punishment is not uniform but depends on the monitoring/punishment network.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that network architecture (who can punish whom) is a critical moderator. In games with complete or well-connected networks, punishment increases contributions and can improve efficiency, especially when punishment expenditures are low. In directed or disconnected networks, punishment is more frequent and severe, often reducing efficiency due to high punishment costs. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR), punishment cost, and other game parameters are held constant, so the findings isolate the effect of network structure. When using control efficiency to predict treatment efficiency, one must account for the network's connectedness, completeness, and directedness, as these determine whether punishment will be efficiency-enhancing or not. The results suggest that simply enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the network context is decisive.

- source: Cobo-Reyes, R., Katz, G., Markussen, T., & Meraglia, S. (2022). Voting on sanctioning institutions in open and closed communities: experimental evidence. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares public goods games with endogenous institutional choice (formal vs. informal sanctions) in closed (fixed group) and open (migration allowed) societies. Formal sanctions always yield higher average payoffs and contributions than informal sanctions, but the gap is much larger in open societies. In closed societies, informal sanctions can sustain high cooperation and efficiency if initial group cooperation is high, but this is undermined when migration is allowed. The popularity and effectiveness of informal sanctions are fragile to group openness. The main efficiency outcome is average individual payoff per period, which is significantly higher under formal sanctions than informal sanctions in open societies (MT: FS=65.59, IS=60.63), but not significantly different in closed societies (NMT: FS=68.29, IS=67.59).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment (specifically, formal/centralized punishment) is larger in open societies (where group membership is endogenous) than in closed societies. When predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, the presence of migration or endogenous group formation is a key moderator: punishment is more effective at raising efficiency when groups are open. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR), punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness are all specified, allowing for precise mapping to prediction dimensions. Control (no-punishment) efficiency is not directly reported, but the comparison between informal and formal sanctions provides a strong basis for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The results are most informative for settings with 5 players, 30 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and the specific punishment/reward technologies described.

- source: Castillo, J. G., Xu, Z. P., Zhang, P., & Zhu, X. C. (2021). The effects of centralized power and institutional legitimacy on collective action. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that centralized punishment institutions (where a single manager can punish group members) significantly increase both contributions and efficiency (net profit) compared to a baseline public goods game without punishment. This result holds across two experimental variants: one with restricted and costly punishment (1:1 cost, only one person can be punished per round) and one with unrestricted and 'cheap' punishment (3:1 cost, multiple people can be punished per round). However, the mechanism by which the manager is selected (random/exogenous vs. by group vote/endogenous) does not significantly affect efficiency or contributions. The efficiency gains are attributed to the presence of punishment, not to the legitimacy conferred by democratic selection. The design also shows that the prosociality of the manager (their prior contribution behavior) is more important for group outcomes than the selection mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear PGG with centralized punishment, enabling punishment increases efficiency (payoff) relative to a no-punishment baseline, regardless of whether the manager is selected by vote or at random. The effect is robust across different punishment cost/effectiveness regimes. For prediction tasks, the presence of a centralized punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, and the details of the manager selection mechanism (democratic vs. exogenous) are not predictive of further efficiency gains. The results also suggest that punishment cost and effectiveness (within the tested range) do not moderate the efficiency effect of punishment in this centralized setting.

- source: Pfattheicher, S., Böhm, R., & Kesberg, R. (2018). The Advantage of Democratic Peer Punishment in Sustaining Cooperation within Groups. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Democratic peer punishment in a repeated public goods game leads to higher cooperation and higher total group payoff than standard peer punishment, primarily by reducing costly and antisocial punishment. Over six rounds, both punishment systems are less efficient than no-punishment, but democratic peer punishment's efficiency increases over time and may eventually exceed the no-punishment baseline. Psychological outcomes (fairness, satisfaction, trust) are also highest in the democratic punishment condition. The study provides direct evidence on how the structure of punishment (democratic vs. standard) moderates the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of the punishment mechanism (specifically, introducing a democratic voting step for punishment execution) can substantially moderate the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in public goods games. When predicting the effect of enabling punishment, it is important to distinguish between standard peer punishment and democratic peer punishment: standard peer punishment increases cooperation but reduces efficiency due to high punishment costs, while democratic peer punishment increases both cooperation and efficiency relative to standard peer punishment, and may eventually outperform no-punishment in efficiency if the game is long enough. The findings are directly relevant for settings with 4 players, 6 rounds, continuous contributions, no chat, and a punishment cost/impact ratio of 1:3. The results suggest that, all else equal, democratic peer punishment is more likely to yield positive or neutral efficiency effects compared to standard peer punishment, especially as the number of rounds increases.

- source: Dorrough, A., Glöckner, A., & Lee, B. (2017). Race for Power in Public Good Games with Unequal, Unstable Punishment Power. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across three lab experiments, the authors show that in standard public good games with punishment, group efficiency is higher when punishment power is equal and stable. When punishment power is both unequal (one strong punisher, three weak) and unstable (the strong role can change based on performance or prosociality), groups experience more punishment, similar or lower contributions, and significantly lower efficiency (average round win). The negative effect on efficiency is robust to whether the strong role is assigned based on selfish or prosocial behavior. The detrimental effect is not due to inequality alone, but to the combination of inequality and instability, which triggers a 'race for power' and resource waste.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public good games with punishment, introducing instability in the assignment of punishment power (especially when combined with inequality in punishment effectiveness) reduces group efficiency compared to stable, equal punishment power. For prediction, if the control (punishment disabled) efficiency is known, enabling punishment with equal, stable power is likely to increase efficiency, but enabling punishment with unequal, unstable power is likely to decrease efficiency relative to the equal, stable punishment case. The effect is driven by increased competitive punishment rather than increased cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for settings with 4 players, 10 rounds, continuous contributions, no chat, and full feedback on others' actions.

- source: Page, T., Putterman, L., & Unel, B. (2005). Voluntary association in public goods experiments: Reciprocity, mimicry and efficiency. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 2x2 lab experiment with baseline, punishment, regrouping, and combined treatments, both punishment and regrouping increase efficiency and contributions relative to baseline. Regrouping (voluntary association) yields higher efficiency than punishment, as punishment incurs direct costs to both punisher and punished. The combined treatment (regrouping + punishment) does not significantly outperform regrouping alone. The experiment also finds strong evidence of conditional cooperation and group sorting by contribution level. Efficiency rates are: Baseline 77%, Regrouping 88%, Punishment 81%, Combined 86%.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with 4 players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and MPCR of 0.4, adding a punishment mechanism (cost 0.25 per 1 unit punished, impact 1 per 0.25 spent) increases efficiency from 77% (baseline) to 81%. However, the efficiency gain is smaller than that from enabling voluntary regrouping (88%). The results suggest that, under these parameters, punishment increases efficiency but the effect is limited by the costliness of punishment. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in similar lab PGGs.

- source: Masclet, D., Noussair, C. N., & Villeval, M. C. (2013). THREAT AND PUNISHMENT IN PUBLIC GOOD EXPERIMENTS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with costly punishment, adding a stage where players can issue non-binding threats to punish (conditioned on others' contribution levels) increases average contributions and, in the latter periods, increases group efficiency (payoff) compared to a baseline with punishment but no threats. However, the cost of punishment initially offsets the benefit of higher contributions, so efficiency only improves in later rounds. If a second-order punishment stage is added (where players can punish those who fail to carry out their threats), threats become less severe, contributions and efficiency fall, and welfare is equal to or below the baseline. The effect of threats is robust to changes in punishment effectiveness, but the efficiency gain is less persistent when punishment is less effective. Overall, threats can improve efficiency, but only under certain conditions and mainly in longer interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with punishment, adding a non-binding threat stage can increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to a baseline with punishment but no threats, but only in the latter periods of repeated play. The efficiency gain is modest and depends on the absence of second-order punishment (punishing those who fail to carry out threats). If second-order punishment is possible, efficiency falls to or below baseline. The effect is also sensitive to punishment effectiveness (punishment technology). For prediction, this suggests that enabling threats (structured, non-binding, and observable) can increase efficiency above the control (punishment-only) baseline, but only in longer games and only if threats are not themselves subject to enforcement. The effect is stronger when punishment is more effective (higher punishment technology). The control efficiency (punishment-only) is a good lower bound; threats can improve on this, but only under the right design conditions.

- source: Lopez, M. C., Murphy, J. J., Spraggon, J. M., & Stranlund, J. K. (2012). COMPARING THE EFFECTIVENESS OF REGULATION AND PRO-SOCIAL EMOTIONS TO ENHANCE COOPERATION: EXPERIMENTAL EVIDENCE FROM FISHING COMMUNITIES IN COLOMBIA. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a framed field experiment with Colombian fishing communities, groups of 5 played a 15-round linear public goods game with a marginal per capita return of 0.4. Treatments included a baseline, a 'frame' (reminder of social optimum), 'guilt' (private feedback), 'shame' (random public revelation of non-cooperation), and two punishment treatments (low and high penalty, both with 1/5 audit probability and per-unit fines). Both punishment treatments increased contributions and expected earnings relative to baseline, but the high-penalty treatment (designed to induce full compliance) resulted in the lowest average expected earnings due to fines. The shame treatment produced the highest contributions and expected earnings, significantly outperforming both punishment treatments. The paper concludes that social mechanisms (shame) can be more effective and efficient than regulatory punishment in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear PGG with 5 players, 15 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and known round count, introducing monetary punishment (with random audits and per-unit fines) increases efficiency relative to baseline, but the effect is limited: high-penalty punishment can reduce efficiency due to fines. The effect of punishment is smaller than that of a social mechanism (random public revelation/shame). For prediction, the paper suggests that punishment will not always increase efficiency above control, especially if fines are high and compliance is imperfect. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, audit probability, punishment cost/tech) are all specified, and the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency under punishment in similar field or framed settings.

- source: Kroll, S., Cherry, T. L., & Shogren, J. F. (2007). Voting, punishment, and public goods. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with five players and repeated rounds, the introduction of a nonbinding voting mechanism alone does not significantly increase efficiency or sustain higher contributions compared to the standard individual contribution baseline. However, when a costly peer punishment mechanism is added—allowing group members to punish those who deviate from the majority-voted contribution plan—both contributions and efficiency increase substantially and persistently. The efficiency in the nonbinding-vote-with-punishment treatment is significantly higher than in the nonbinding-vote treatment and approaches the efficiency observed in the binding-vote (third-party enforced) treatment. The cost of punishment is more than offset by the gains in group payoff, so overall efficiency rises with punishment enabled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with five players, 10 rounds per stage, and a marginal per-capita return of 0.4, enabling costly peer punishment in a nonbinding voting environment leads to a large and persistent increase in efficiency compared to the same game without punishment. The effect is robust and the efficiency gain outweighs the cost of punishment. Thus, when predicting the efficiency of a public goods game with punishment enabled, this paper supports a substantial positive adjustment relative to the control (no-punishment) efficiency, provided the punishment mechanism is similar (costly, peer-administered, and targeted at non-cooperators). The findings are directly relevant for games with similar design parameters.

- source: Peng, H. C. (2022). Punishment mechanisms and cooperation in public goods games: Experimental evidence. *Annals of Public and Cooperative Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab public goods game with four players per group, the introduction of either individual or majority-voting punishment mechanisms increases average contributions relative to the no-punishment baseline, regardless of whether MPCRs are homogeneous or heterogeneous. However, group payoffs (efficiency) do not significantly differ between the no-punishment, individual punishment, and majority-voting punishment conditions. The cost of punishment offsets the gains from increased cooperation. The majority-voting punishment mechanism reduces the frequency of antisocial punishment compared to individual punishment, particularly in homogeneous groups, but antisocial punishment remains present in all conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with four players, six rounds per condition, and either homogeneous (MPCR=0.5) or heterogeneous (MPCR=0.3/0.5/0.7) returns, enabling punishment (either individual or majority-voting) increases contributions but does not increase efficiency relative to the control. The cost of punishment absorbs the surplus from higher cooperation. Thus, when predicting efficiency in similar lab PGGs, the presence of punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency unless the cost structure or punishment technology is more favorable. The majority-voting mechanism may reduce antisocial punishment but does not improve efficiency over individual punishment. The findings are most applicable to small, anonymous, stranger-matched groups with no communication and known round structure.

- source: Traulsen, A., Röhl, T., & Milinski, M. (2012). An economic experiment reveals that humans prefer pool punishment to maintain the commons. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated public goods games with five players per group, the introduction of pool punishment (especially with second-order punishment) increases cooperation and reduces defection, but leads to a significant loss in efficiency (average payoff per round drops by about a third). Peer punishment alone does not significantly affect efficiency, as punishment is rarely used when cooperation is high. When both peer and pool punishment are available, pool punishment dominates in the presence of second-order punishment, again with a substantial efficiency loss. The results support the theoretical prediction that pool punishment can stabilize cooperation but at the cost of group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard public goods game with five players, 25 rounds, no communication, binary (all-or-nothing) contributions, and known punishment costs and effectiveness, enabling pool punishment (especially with second-order punishment) reduces efficiency compared to the control (no or only peer punishment). The efficiency loss is substantial (about one third) when pool punishment is used with second-order punishment. Peer punishment alone does not significantly reduce efficiency. Thus, for prediction tasks, the presence and type of punishment mechanism (peer vs pool), and whether second-order punishment is implemented, are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. The specific parameterization (punishment cost, punishment impact, MPCR) is also informative for quantitative prediction.

- source: Egas, M., & Riedl, A. (2008). The economics of altruistic punishment and the maintenance of cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment only maintains cooperation when it is relatively effective (low cost to punisher, high impact on punished). However, even when cooperation is sustained, group and individual payoffs are lower than in the control without punishment, because the costs of punishment exceed the benefits from increased cooperation. The threshold of free-riding that goes unpunished increases as punishment becomes less effective. The main determinant of punishment's effect is the cost-to-impact ratio. The results suggest that altruistic punishment alone is insufficient to maintain cooperation efficiently, as it reduces group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard PGG with anonymous, one-shot (perfect stranger) interactions, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency and can reduce it, especially when punishment is effective (low cost, high impact). The effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the cost-to-impact ratio: only when punishment is cheap and effective does it sustain cooperation, but even then, group payoffs are lower than in the no-punishment control. For prediction, this suggests that unless punishment is both cheap and highly effective, enabling punishment will not improve and may reduce efficiency relative to control. The control efficiency (without punishment) is always higher than with punishment in this design. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions, especially punishment cost and effectiveness.

- source: Markussen, T., Putterman, L., & Tyran, J. R. (2014). Self-Organization for Collective Action: An Experimental Study of Voting on Sanction Regimes. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with 5 players, groups could vote between no sanctions (NS), informal sanctions (IS: peer punishment), and formal sanctions (FS: centralized punishment with fixed and variable costs, deterrent or non-deterrent). Both IS and deterrent FS substantially increased group efficiency and earnings relative to NS. IS were highly cost-effective, yielding higher returns per point spent on sanctions than FS in most conditions. IS were especially effective when chosen by vote, with group earnings and efficiency about 30% higher than when IS were imposed exogenously. Non-deterrent FS also increased efficiency when chosen by vote, but less so than IS. High fixed costs for FS reduced both their efficiency and their popularity. Reducing the effectiveness of IS (increasing cost per unit punishment) decreased their efficiency somewhat, but IS remained popular and relatively effective. Allowing IS and FS to coexist did not substantially change these patterns. Overall, IS can be as or more effective than FS in promoting efficiency, especially when adopted democratically.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with 5 players, enabling informal peer punishment (IS) can increase group efficiency to levels comparable to or exceeding those achieved by formal sanctions, especially when IS are chosen by vote. The efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost-effectiveness of the sanction regime, the fixed and variable costs of punishment, and whether the regime is adopted endogenously. High fixed costs for FS reduce their efficiency advantage. IS are most effective when punishment is relatively cheap and well-targeted. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling IS is likely to yield a substantial increase in efficiency over the no-punishment baseline, and that the effect is robust to moderate changes in punishment technology and institutional coexistence. The control (no-sanction) efficiency is typically well below full cooperation, while IS and deterrent FS can approach or reach high efficiency, with IS often being more cost-effective.

- source: Kamei, K. (2019). The power of joint decision-making in a finitely-repeated dilemma. *Oxford Economic Papers-new Series*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Joint decision-making in pairs, where members can communicate, leads to higher and more sustained efficiency (measured by average group payoff/contributions) than individual decision-making in finitely repeated two-player public goods games. This effect is strongest under partner matching (fixed pairs), where efficiency remains high until the end-game. Under stranger matching (random rematching), efficiency declines more rapidly, but is still higher than individual decision-making in early rounds if social distance is close. When compared to individual decision-making with informal punishment (where subjects can punish at a cost), joint decision-making achieves similar efficiency and average payoffs. The effect of joint decision-making is driven by communication and, to a lesser extent, by close social distance within pairs. Removing social distance (anonymous pairs) reduces efficiency, especially under stranger matching. The paper provides direct evidence on the efficiency impact of punishment institutions versus joint decision-making, and indirect evidence on the role of communication, matching protocol, and social distance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player finitely repeated public goods games, enabling informal punishment (with a cost to the punisher and a larger cost to the punished) increases efficiency to a level comparable to that achieved by joint decision-making in pairs with communication. The efficiency boost from punishment is robust under both partner and stranger matching, though the effect is stronger under partner matching. The design dimensions most relevant for predicting efficiency with punishment enabled are: player count (2), number of rounds (15), MPCR (0.8), punishment cost (0.5 per point), punishment tech (1.5 per point), and matching protocol (partner vs stranger). Communication within pairs (chat) is a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially under partner matching. The paper's direct comparison of control (no punishment), punishment, and joint decision-making treatments makes it highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Chaudhuri, A., & Paichayontvijit, T. (2017). On the long-run efficacy of punishments and recommendations in a laboratory public goods game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In repeated linear public goods games with groups of four, both peer punishment and exhortative recommendations (public messages) increase efficiency compared to a control with no intervention. Recommendations have a strong and immediate positive effect on efficiency, especially with fixed group composition (partners), but this effect decays over time. Punishment starts with lower efficiency due to the cost of punishing but shows an upward trend over time, eventually catching up to or surpassing recommendations in some conditions, particularly with longer time horizons. However, in these experiments, punishment never outperforms the restart recommendation treatment. The effect of punishment is stronger with fixed matching than with random rematching. The cost-effectiveness of punishment depends on the punishment technology (cost/impact ratio) and the length of the game. The study provides direct evidence on how efficiency changes when punishment is enabled, with detailed efficiency data for control, recommendation, and punishment treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in standard linear public goods games. It reports efficiency (average earnings as a fraction of the social optimum) for control and punishment treatments, with and without recommendations, across different matching protocols and time horizons. The results show that punishment can increase efficiency over time, but the effect is not immediate and depends on the number of rounds, matching protocol, and punishment cost/impact. The efficiency gain from punishment is more pronounced with longer games and fixed matching. The paper also provides detailed information on the punishment cost and technology, allowing for direct mapping to prediction dimensions. For prediction, the control efficiency and the game design dimensions (player count, rounds, matching, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are all reported, making this paper a strong source for model training and validation.

- source: De Geest, L. R., & Kingsley, D. C. (2019). Endowment heterogeneity, incomplete information & institutional choice in public good experiments. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with three treatments (equal endowments, heterogeneous observed endowments, and heterogeneous unobserved endowments), the introduction of peer punishment increases group payoffs only when endowments are either equal or observed. When endowments are unobserved, peer punishment leads to lower group payoffs than the no-punishment baseline, due to mis-targeted punishment (cooperators being punished, freeriders escaping punishment). Central authority enforcement, which perfectly enforces the social optimum but at a fixed cost, yields similar payoffs across all treatments and is preferred by groups with incomplete information, especially by low-endowment members. The study demonstrates that the efficiency gains from punishment depend critically on the ability to observe and target deviations from contribution norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency effect of enabling punishment in public goods games depends on whether group members can observe the relevant information (here, endowments) needed to target freeriders. When such information is available, punishment increases efficiency; when it is not, punishment can reduce efficiency below the baseline. The control efficiency (no punishment) is similar across treatments, but the treatment efficiency (with punishment) diverges depending on information structure. For prediction, the presence of incomplete information about contribution capacity (endowments) is a key moderator: with incomplete information, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency; with complete information, it is likely to increase efficiency. Other design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, punishment cost) are held constant in this study, so the main moderator identified is information about endowments.

- source: Lowen, A., & Schmitt, P. (2013). Cooperation limitations under a one-time threat of expulsion and punishment. *Journal of Socio-economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a one-time punishment opportunity after a one-time expulsion vote in a 15-round public goods game increases contributions before the punishment phase, but does not improve overall efficiency or average earnings compared to expulsion alone. The threat of punishment raises the standard for avoiding expulsion, leading to more expulsions, including of moderate contributors. The costs associated with punishment (both direct and through increased expulsions) offset the gains from higher contributions, resulting in nearly identical efficiency between the two treatments. Thus, in this design, adding a costly punishment mechanism to a single expulsion opportunity does not yield net efficiency gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with a single expulsion opportunity, adding a one-time, costly punishment mechanism increases contributions but does not increase efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, under similar game parameters (large group, 15 rounds, MPCR 0.2, costly punishment, no communication), the efficiency with punishment enabled will be very similar to the efficiency with punishment disabled, as the costs of punishment offset the increased contributions. The findings are most applicable to settings with infrequent, costly punishment and expulsion opportunities, and may not generalize to games with frequent or low-cost punishment.

- source: Gross, J., De Dreu, C. K. W., & Reddmann, L. (2022). Shadow of conflict: How past conflict influences group cooperation and the use of punishment. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a lab experiment with groups of four, participants either experienced an asymmetric intergroup conflict (attacker-defender game) or not, and then played a repeated public goods game with peer punishment. The main findings are: (1) Prior conflict does not significantly reduce average cooperation rates, but does reduce group earnings (efficiency) due to less effective punishment. (2) When previous conflict roles are identifiable, punishment is less likely to be targeted at free-riders and is less effective at increasing cooperation, especially when administered by previous attackers. (3) Hiding previous conflict roles (resetting group tags) increases both cooperation and group earnings over time, and punishment regains its effectiveness. (4) The negative effect of conflict on efficiency is not due to lower cooperation per se, but to maladaptive punishment patterns that reduce the welfare gains from cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with peer punishment, the presence of a prior asymmetric conflict (with identifiable roles) reduces group efficiency (earnings) even if average cooperation rates are not significantly lower. The reduction in efficiency is due to less effective punishment: free-riders are punished less, and punishment by previous attackers is less effective. If prior conflict roles are hidden, efficiency increases and punishment regains its effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency is moderated by the social context—specifically, whether there is a 'shadow of conflict' and whether past roles are identifiable. In standard PGG designs without such conflict history, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, identifiability) are all well specified, and the control efficiency is directly comparable.

- source: Ertör-Akyazi, P. (2019). Formal versus informal institutions: Extraction and earnings in framed field experiments with small-scale fishing communities in Turkey. *Marine Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a framed field experiment with Turkish small-scale fishers, four treatments were compared: baseline (open-access), face-to-face communication, monetary penalty (punishment for exceeding quota), and purchase of fishing rights (market-based over-quota payment). All groups played a linear PGG extraction game for 15 rounds (pre-treatment), then 14 rounds under one of the four treatments. The monetary penalty (punishment) significantly reduced extraction and moderately increased group earnings compared to baseline, but the effect was smaller than that of communication, which yielded the highest earnings and lowest inequality. The purchase of fishing rights (identical cost to penalty) did not significantly reduce extraction or increase earnings. Thus, punishment can improve efficiency, but its effect is sensitive to framing and is less than that of informal communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing a monetary penalty (punishment) in a linear PGG extraction game with small groups (n=5) and low MPCR (0.4) increases group efficiency (earnings) relative to open-access, but the effect is moderate. The effect is smaller than that of enabling face-to-face communication. The framing of the punishment matters: a market-based 'purchase of rights' with identical cost is less effective than a penalty. For prediction, this suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency over baseline, but the magnitude depends on the punishment's framing and the presence of communication. Control efficiency in this context is already above the selfish prediction, so the incremental gain from punishment is moderate. The results are most applicable to small groups, continuous extraction, no chat in baseline/punishment, and low but certain punishment.

- source: Nicklisch, A., Putterman, L., & Thöni, C. (2021). Trigger-happy or precisionist? On demand for monitoring in peer-based public goods provision. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the ability to monitor others' contributions at a modest cost enables peer punishment to sustain high cooperation and efficiency in public goods games, even when information is otherwise imperfect. When monitoring is cheap, groups achieve high efficiency, with average earnings close to the efficient benchmark. When monitoring is expensive, efficiency drops significantly. Publicly revealing monitoring decisions does not significantly affect efficiency compared to private monitoring. The main moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency is the cost of monitoring, not the publicness of monitoring. Groups with higher average monitoring achieve substantially higher efficiency. Punishment is most effective when informed by accurate monitoring, and the threat of punishment (signaled by monitoring) can deter free riding, reducing the need for actual punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling punishment in public goods games depend critically on the cost and availability of accurate information about others' contributions. If monitoring is cheap, punishment opportunities lead to high efficiency; if monitoring is expensive, efficiency gains are much smaller. The publicness of monitoring (whether others know who is monitoring) has little effect on efficiency. For prediction, the key design dimensions are the cost of monitoring (a proxy for information quality), the presence and effectiveness of punishment, and the marginal per-capita return. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the paper's results show that punishment with affordable monitoring can raise efficiency close to the social optimum. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially in settings where information about contributions is imperfect but can be improved at a cost.

- source: Ambrus, A., & Greiner, B. (2019). Individual, Dictator, and Democratic punishment in public good games with perfect and imperfect observability. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper experimentally compares three punishment institutions (individual, democratic, dictator) in repeated 5-person public goods games, under both perfect and imperfect monitoring. Democratic punishment, where a majority vote is required to punish, results in significantly higher average net profits (efficiency) and higher contributions than individual or dictator punishment. This is true in both monitoring conditions, though the efficiency advantage is somewhat reduced under noise. The improvement is due to a sharp reduction in anti-social punishment (punishing cooperators), while pro-social punishment (punishing defectors) is maintained. Dictator punishment does not outperform individual punishment in terms of efficiency. The study provides direct, quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment institution and monitoring noise on group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated public goods games. It shows that the institutional design of punishment (democratic vs. individual vs. dictator) and the observability of contributions (perfect vs. noisy) are key moderators. Democratic punishment substantially increases efficiency compared to individual punishment, especially by reducing anti-social punishment. However, the efficiency gain is somewhat sensitive to monitoring noise. The paper provides exact values for average net profits under each condition, allowing for quantitative calibration of prediction models. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, observability, etc.) are all clearly specified, making the results highly transferable to similar prediction tasks.

- source: Kölle, F. (2015). Heterogeneity and cooperation: The role of capability and valuation on public goods provision. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This experiment compares three public goods game treatments: homogeneous groups, groups with one high-valuation member (valuation heterogeneity), and groups with one high-capability member (capability heterogeneity). Each group plays 20 rounds, with punishment enabled in rounds 11-20. Efficiency (group payoff as % of social optimum) is measured with and without punishment. In homogeneous groups, punishment does not significantly change efficiency. In valuation-heterogeneous groups, punishment does not significantly increase efficiency and increases inequality. In capability-heterogeneous groups, punishment significantly increases efficiency (from 86% to 93% of optimum) and reduces inequality. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency is thus conditional on the type of heterogeneity, being most effective when group members differ in capability but not in valuation.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect is not uniform but depends on the type of group heterogeneity. If the game is homogeneous or has only capability heterogeneity (where some players' contributions are more productive but all value the public good equally), enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency, especially in the capability-heterogeneous case. If the group has valuation heterogeneity (where some players value the public good more), punishment is less effective or even counterproductive for efficiency and increases inequality. The control efficiency (without punishment) is also higher in capability-heterogeneous groups than in valuation-heterogeneous groups. Thus, when using game design dimensions and control efficiency to predict treatment efficiency, the type of heterogeneity (capability vs. valuation) is a critical moderator. Other design features (e.g., punishment cost, group size, feedback) are held constant in this study.

- source: Nikiforakis, N., & Engelmann, D. (2011). Altruistic punishment and the threat of feuds. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This experiment compares three public goods game treatments: (1) Baseline (B), where punishment is possible but cannot be avenged (single punishment stage, random IDs each round); (2) Short Feuds (SF), where multiple punishment stages are possible within a round but IDs are randomized each round (so feuds cannot span rounds); and (3) Long Feuds (LF), where multiple punishment stages are possible and IDs are fixed, so feuds can span rounds. The study finds that as the threat of feuds increases (from B to SF to LF), the frequency of altruistic punishment decreases, and so does the threat of punishment for free-riding. In LF, where feuds are most possible, both cooperation and group earnings decline over time relative to the baseline. Severe punishment is used more in SF to preempt counter-punishment, but in LF, punishment is rare and less targeted at free riders. Overall, the possibility of feuds undermines the efficiency gains from punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on whether punishment can be avenged (i.e., whether feuds are possible). In standard designs where punishment is one-shot and anonymous, punishment can increase or maintain efficiency. However, when punishment can be countered and feuds can develop (especially across rounds), the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is greatly reduced or even reversed, as both cooperation and group earnings decline. For prediction, the presence of counter-punishment opportunities and the ability to identify punishers are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is higher than with punishment when feuds are possible, due to the breakdown of cooperation and increased costs from feuding.

- source: Kube, S., Schaube, S., Schildberg-Hörisch, H., & Khachatryan, E. (2015). Institution formation and cooperation with heterogeneous agents. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with three players, the introduction of an institution formation stage (via unanimity voting) that, if implemented, imposes binding contribution obligations enforced by deterrent sanctions, leads to large increases in efficiency (group payoff as a fraction of the maximum possible). For homogeneous groups, a symmetric institution (equal obligations) is almost always implemented and achieves efficiency above 90%. For heterogeneous groups, a symmetric institution is often rejected by low-benefit players due to inequality aversion, but an asymmetric institution (with obligations tailored to equalize payoffs) is much more likely to be implemented, though at a cost to total efficiency. In all cases, efficiency is higher when the institution is implemented than when it is not. The main moderators are the distribution of marginal per-capita returns (homogeneous vs. heterogeneous) and the symmetry of institutional obligations. The study does not manipulate punishment cost or technology directly, as the institution, if implemented, is enforced with sufficiently strong sanctions to ensure compliance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with three players and 20 rounds, the introduction of a binding, deterrent-sanction institution (enabled by unanimity voting) increases efficiency relative to the baseline game without such an institution. The effect is strongest when the institution is both efficient and equitable (i.e., symmetric for homogeneous groups, asymmetric for heterogeneous groups). When the institution is rejected (often due to inequality aversion), efficiency is lower, but still generally higher than in the baseline. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a strong, binding punishment institution (with high compliance) should be expected to increase efficiency, but the magnitude depends on group heterogeneity and the design of institutional obligations. The study does not provide evidence on the effect of varying punishment cost or technology, as these are not manipulated; the punishment regime is always fully deterrent. Control efficiency (without institution) is reported and can be used as a baseline for prediction.

- source: Bochet, O., & Putterman, L. (2009). Not just babble: Opening the black box of communication in a voluntary contribution experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of four, the addition of a costly punishment stage increases contributions but not efficiency, as punishment costs offset the gains. Allowing non-binding numerical announcements or promises alone does not improve efficiency or contributions. However, when the ability to make non-binding promises is combined with punishment, both contributions and group earnings (efficiency) increase significantly. This is because the threat of punishment for broken promises makes communication credible, reducing the frequency of lying and the need for actual costly punishment. Thus, the combination of communication (promises) and punishment is more effective for efficiency than punishment alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with four players, 10 rounds, and a marginal per capita return of 0.4, the addition of costly punishment increases contributions but not efficiency, due to the cost of punishment. However, if the game also allows for non-binding promise statements (in addition to punishment and numerical communication), efficiency increases significantly, as credible communication reduces the need for costly punishment. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the communication environment: punishment alone does not improve efficiency, but punishment plus credible communication (promises) does. The paper also provides detailed evidence on the cost structure of punishment and the behavioral mechanisms involved.

- source: Fehr, E., Fischbacher, U., & Gächter, S. (2002). Strong reciprocity, human cooperation, and the enforcement of social norms. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In repeated public goods games with and without punishment, the introduction of a costly punishment option leads to a dramatic increase in cooperation and a 40% increase in aggregate monetary payoff, even after subtracting the costs of punishment. Without punishment, cooperation and efficiency unravel over time, with most subjects defecting by the end. With punishment, cooperation is sustained or even increases, and group earnings approach the social optimum. The effect is observed both in stable groups (partner design) and in randomly re-matched groups (stranger design), though the effect is stronger in stable groups. The paper also shows that punishment is targeted at defectors and is proportional to the degree of free-riding. The findings are robust across cultures and with high monetary stakes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence that enabling costly punishment in a standard public goods game with known MPCR, group size, and round structure leads to a large, positive effect on group efficiency compared to the same game without punishment. The effect is robust to group composition (fixed or random), and the paper provides enough detail on the game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, information structure) to inform predictions for similar designs.
