The findings support the prediction that, controlling for control-game efficiency, enabling punishment will substantially increase efficiency in public goods game-like environments, especially when punishment is sufficiently effective and observable.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both informal (peer) and formal (rule-based) sanctions increase group efficiency in public goods games relative to a no-sanction baseline. When formal sanctions have no fixed cost, they are more popular and at least as efficient as informal sanctions. However, when formal sanctions require a fixed cost, groups often choose informal sanctions, which then deliver higher efficiency than formal sanctions with a fixed cost. Over time, experience with informal sanctions leads to improved targeting and deterrence, further increasing efficiency. Endogenous (voted) choice of sanctioning regime, especially informal sanctions, results in higher efficiency than exogenously imposed regimes, likely due to self-selection and signaling effects. The study also finds that the effectiveness of informal sanctions is robust to allowing counter-punishment (identifying punishers), and that both types of sanctions can make cooperation individually rational by reversing the incentive to free ride.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (either informal or formal) in a standard public goods game increases efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is especially strong for informal sanctions when formal sanctions are costly. The efficiency gain from punishment is moderated by the cost structure of the punishment institution (fixed and variable costs), the endogenous choice of institution, and experience over repeated rounds. For prediction, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially if chosen by vote and with low or no fixed cost) should be expected to increase efficiency substantially, often approaching the full-cooperation benchmark. The findings also suggest that the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), punishment cost, and whether punishment is formal or informal are key moderators. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is much lower than with punishment enabled, and the difference is largest when punishment is well-targeted and not too costly.

- source: Engelmann, D., & Nikiforakis, N. (2015). In the long-run we are all dead: on the benefits of peer punishment in rich environments. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with 4 players and 30 rounds, the authors compare three treatments: (1) No Punishment (NP), (2) Standard Punishment (SP, one anonymous punishment stage per round, IDs randomized each round), and (3) Rich Punishment (RP, multiple punishment stages per round, fixed IDs, full information, allowing for retaliation and complex strategies). In SP, group earnings increase significantly over time and surpass NP in the second half of the experiment. In RP, group earnings do not increase significantly over time and are not significantly different from NP, even in later rounds. Contributions are higher in both punishment treatments than in NP, but only in SP do they increase over time. In RP, some groups achieve full cooperation, but others experience breakdowns and feuds, leading to high punishment costs and no net efficiency gain. The results suggest that the efficiency of peer punishment is highly sensitive to the structure of the punishment environment, especially the possibility of retaliation and the richness of available strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency effect of enabling peer punishment in public goods games depends on the design of the punishment mechanism. When punishment is limited to a single, anonymous stage per round (SP), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control. When punishment is unconstrained and allows for retaliation (RP), enabling punishment does not increase efficiency relative to the control, despite higher contributions, due to offsetting punishment costs and feuding. For prediction, the presence of multiple punishment stages, fixed IDs, and full information about who punished whom are critical moderators that can nullify the efficiency gains of punishment. Control efficiency (NP) is similar across treatments, but the treatment effect of punishment depends on these design features.

- source: Denant-Boemont, L., Masclet, D., & Noussair, C. N. (2007). Punishment, counterpunishment and sanction enforcement in a social dilemma experiment. *Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly sensitive to the design of the punishment mechanism. In the Baseline (single-stage punishment) treatment, efficiency is high: contributions and earnings are close to the social optimum. When a second stage of punishment is added and counterpunishment is possible, contributions and welfare fall significantly. The positive effect of sanction enforcement (punishing those who fail to punish free riders) is present but smaller than the negative effect of counterpunishment. In the most extreme case, with five rounds of punishment (6SFI), both contributions and welfare are significantly lower than in the Baseline, and welfare is even lower than in the no-punishment scenario. The results show that punishment can backfire and reduce efficiency if it enables cycles of retaliation (counterpunishment) or is too unconstrained.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling punishment in public goods games depend critically on the details of the punishment system. If punishment is anonymous and limited to a single round, it increases efficiency. If counterpunishment is possible, or if there are multiple rounds of punishment, efficiency can decrease, sometimes below the no-punishment baseline. For prediction, the presence of counterpunishment opportunities, the number of punishment rounds, and the information structure (anonymity of punishers) are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is high in this design, and adding punishment can either increase or decrease efficiency depending on these moderators.

- source: Rockenbach, B., & Wolff, I. (2016). Designing Institutions for Social Dilemmas. *German Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that when subjects are allowed to design institutions for a repeated public goods game, they initially favor punishment or redistribution mechanisms, but these are associated with lower efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) compared to rules that emphasize communication (framing, appeals) or participative elements (endogenous rule change). Over the course of the experiment, the use of punishment declines, and efficiency increases, suggesting that punishment is not an efficient solution in this context. Communication and participative rule features are positively associated with efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that punishment, as typically implemented by endogenous designers, tends to reduce efficiency, mainly due to its costs, and that designers learn to move away from it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with endogenous institution design, enabling punishment mechanisms tends to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) baseline, especially when punishment is costly and not peer-administered. The effect is robust across multiple rounds and rule sets, with efficiency increasing as designers move away from punishment toward communication and participative mechanisms. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially costly, centralized, or automatic forms) is likely to result in lower efficiency than the control, unless the punishment is highly refined or replaced by redistribution mechanisms with lower waste. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in similar PGG environments.

- source: Gintis, H., Bowles, S., Boyd, R., & Fehr, E. (2003). Explaining altruistic behavior in humans. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Experimental public goods games consistently show that when participants can punish defectors at a personal cost, cooperation rates remain high or even increase over time, and group payoffs (efficiency) are substantially higher than when punishment is not allowed. In the Fehr & Gächter (2000) experiments, for example, allowing punishment led to a 40% increase in net payoffs to all subjects, even after accounting for the costs of punishment. The effect is observed even under strict anonymity and in one-shot or non-repeated interactions, indicating that the mechanism is not driven by expectations of future reciprocity. The paper also reviews cross-cultural evidence and evolutionary models, but the core experimental result is that punishment opportunities robustly improve group efficiency in public goods game environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, direct evidence that enabling costly punishment in public goods games increases group efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The effect is robust across standard lab PGG designs with 4 players, 10 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and known MPCR. The magnitude of the efficiency gain is substantial (e.g., 40% increase in net payoffs in Fehr & Gächter 2000). The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency given the control game design and efficiency, especially in standard PGG settings. The paper also discusses the importance of group structure (e.g., partner vs. stranger matching), but the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is consistent across these variations.

- source: Kosfeld, M., Okada, A., & Riedl, A. (2009). Institution Formation in Public Goods Games. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when groups can endogenously form a centralized sanctioning institution (which punishes non-contributors among its members), they do so in a large majority of cases, and most often as a 'grand organization' including all players. This leads to much higher and more stable contributions and efficiency compared to control groups without the institution option. In the lower MPCR treatment (a=0.4), efficiency rises from 25% (control) to 51% (institution formation); in the higher MPCR treatment (a=0.65), efficiency rises from 62% (control) to 70% (institution formation), though the latter difference is not statistically significant. The institution is only implemented if all players join, reflecting strong fairness concerns. When institution formation fails, efficiency is not lower than in the control. The results show that endogenous punishment institutions can solve the public goods dilemma and raise efficiency, but their success depends on fairness-driven willingness to include all group members.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of enabling centralized punishment institutions in linear public goods games. When the control (no punishment) efficiency is low, enabling institution formation can more than double efficiency, especially at lower MPCR. The effect is robust to group size (n=4), number of rounds (20), and the specific centralized punishment structure used here. The main moderator is the MPCR: the efficiency gain is larger when MPCR is lower. The institution is only effective if all players join, so settings that facilitate or require universal participation are more likely to see large efficiency gains. The cost of the institution (here, 2 points per round shared among members) is low relative to the efficiency gains. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency in similar lab PGGs with centralized punishment options.

- source: Waichman, I., & Stenzel, L. (2019). When Punishment Strikes Late: The Effect of a Delay in Punishment and Punishment Feedback on Cooperation and Efficiency. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that peer punishment increases both cooperation and efficiency in a linear public good game compared to a no-punishment baseline. This effect holds even when punishment is delayed by five periods, as long as participants receive salient feedback linking punishment to their past contributions. If this feedback is absent (i.e., participants are punished after a delay and are not reminded of their past behavior), punishment still increases cooperation but does not improve efficiency over the no-punishment baseline, due to increased punishment costs and less targeted norm enforcement. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency thus depends on the informational environment, specifically whether punished individuals can clearly associate punishment with their own prior actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear PGG with four players and 20 rounds, enabling peer punishment increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, provided that feedback links punishment to past contributions. The effect is robust to a five-period delay in punishment, as long as feedback is salient. If the design omits such feedback, punishment may still increase cooperation but not efficiency, due to higher punishment costs and less effective norm targeting. For prediction, the presence or absence of feedback linking punishment to behavior is a critical moderator. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is lower than treatment efficiency (with punishment and feedback), but not when feedback is absent. Other design dimensions (player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are held constant in this study, so the main moderator evidenced is the feedback/identification of norm violation.

- source: Duell, D., Mengel, F., Mohlin, E., & Weidenholzer, S. (2024). Cooperation through collective punishment and participation. *Political Science Research and Methods*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with imperfect monitoring (noisy signals about individual contributions, but perfect information about total group contribution), the authors experimentally compare five regimes: no punishment, peer-to-peer punishment, collective punishment, peer-to-peer punishment with commitment, and collective punishment with commitment. The key finding is that only collective punishment with commitment (where group members can opt in to a regime that automatically punishes the whole group if total contributions fall short) leads to significantly higher group payoffs and cooperation rates compared to both no punishment and peer-to-peer punishment. Peer-to-peer punishment does not improve payoffs or cooperation relative to no punishment in this noisy environment. The positive effect of collective punishment with commitment is especially pronounced in the later rounds, suggesting a learning or coordination effect. The study also shows that the ability to participate in the decision to implement collective punishment (i.e., voting/commitment) is crucial for its effectiveness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of enabling collective punishment with commitment in a repeated public goods game with imperfect monitoring. If the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling collective punishment with commitment is likely to increase efficiency (group payoff) substantially, especially over time, provided the punishment is collective, pre-committed, and participation in the institution is possible. The effect is not observed for peer-to-peer punishment or for collective punishment without commitment. The experimental design and reporting allow for direct mapping to prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, given the game design dimensions.

- source: Kingsley, D. C., & Brown, T. C. (2016). Endogenous and costly institutional deterrence in a public good experiment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a linear public goods game with a central authority that can monitor and punish free-riders, increasing the probability of monitoring (deterrence) increases contributions up to a point, but the benefit to group earnings depends on the cost of monitoring. When monitoring is cheap or moderate, higher deterrence increases both contributions and efficiency (group earnings as a share of the social optimum). When monitoring is expensive, the cost can outweigh the benefit, and efficiency does not improve or may decline. Allowing groups to endogenously select the level of monitoring does not increase contributions or efficiency at non-deterrent (low monitoring) levels, contrary to some previous literature. In fact, endogenous selection of non-deterrent institutions sometimes reduces contributions. The relationship between monitoring, punishment, and efficiency is thus strongly moderated by the costliness of institutional enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-enabled institutions on efficiency in public goods games, especially when the punishment is implemented via a central authority with costly monitoring. The results show that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on both the probability of monitoring (deterrence) and the cost of monitoring. At low and moderate costs, enabling punishment increases efficiency, but at high costs, the efficiency gain is negated or reversed. The endogenous selection of non-deterrent institutions does not provide an efficiency boost. For prediction, the key moderators are the cost of monitoring (punishment_cost), the effectiveness of monitoring (punishment_tech), and whether the institution is deterrent (i.e., the expected cost of free-riding exceeds the benefit). Control efficiency (without punishment) is much lower than with effective, affordable punishment, but high punishment costs can erase the efficiency gain.

- source: Bühren, C., & Dannenberg, A. (2021). The demand for punishment to promote cooperation among like-minded people. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games is highly contingent on group type and information. Highly cooperative groups achieve near-optimal payoffs without punishment, and introducing punishment reduces efficiency due to its cost. Uncooperative groups only benefit from severe punishment if they are informed about their own composition and implement the institution early; otherwise, punishment is often misapplied or subverted, leading to low efficiency. Middle cooperators benefit from punishment only in the absence of information about group composition. Information about group cooperativeness allows groups to better match their institution choice to their needs, increasing overall efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment is not uniform and is moderated by group composition and information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public goods games is not a simple function of the game design dimensions alone, but is critically moderated by group composition (cooperativeness) and information about that composition. For prediction tasks, this means that the same punishment institution can have positive, negative, or null effects on efficiency depending on whether the group is composed of high, middle, or low cooperators, and whether they know this. In the absence of such information, punishment is often over- or under-used, reducing efficiency. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction from this paper are the presence and cost of punishment, the severity/technology of punishment, the availability of information about group composition, and the baseline cooperativeness of the group. Control efficiency (no punishment) is a strong predictor: if control efficiency is high, adding punishment reduces efficiency; if control efficiency is low, adding severe punishment with information can increase efficiency. The paper also highlights the importance of comprehension and early institution choice for realizing efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Dai, Z. X., Hogarth, R. M., & Villeval, M. C. (2015). Ambiguity on audits and cooperation in a public goods game. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with centralized sanctions triggered by audits, the study manipulates the frequency and regularity of audits (continuous vs. intermittent) and the severity of sanctions. Under ambiguity (unknown audit probability), intermittent audits with high fines (but lower frequency) produce similar or higher contributions and significantly higher efficiency (group payoffs) than continuous audits with low fines. After audits are withdrawn, groups exposed to intermittent audits maintain higher cooperation and efficiency for longer, as their beliefs about audit probability decay more slowly. High frequency-high severity (continuous audits with high fines) also sustains cooperation and efficiency, but at higher cost. Reducing audit frequency or sanction severity too much undermines these effects. The results suggest that, for a given expected cost of sanction, intermittent audits with high severity are more efficient than continuous audits with low severity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a 3-player, 50-round linear PGG with centralized punishment, intermittent (irregular) audit schemes with high severity (punishment effectiveness) and sufficient frequency lead to higher efficiency (group payoffs) than continuous audit schemes with lower severity, especially after audits are withdrawn. The effect is robust as long as audit frequency and severity are not too low. For prediction, this suggests that, holding control efficiency and other game dimensions constant, enabling punishment via intermittent, ambiguous audits with high severity will increase efficiency more than continuous, predictable audits with low severity. The paper also shows that both audit frequency and punishment effectiveness (severity) are key moderators: too few audits or too low severity reduces the efficiency benefit. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in PGG-like environments with centralized punishment.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: This survey synthesizes experimental evidence on the effects of punishment in linear public goods games. It finds that costly punishment opportunities generally increase contributions and cooperation, but do not always increase efficiency or group earnings. Efficiency improvements are only robust when punishment is highly effective (i.e., each unit of punishment inflicts a much larger cost on the target than on the punisher) or when the game is played for many rounds, allowing cooperation to stabilize and punishment costs to fall. In many standard settings, punishment increases cooperation but reduces efficiency due to the costliness of punishment. The presence of anti-social punishment (punishing high contributors) and counter-punishment can further undermine efficiency. The effect of punishment is also moderated by group composition, information, and cultural context.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this survey provides strong evidence that the effect is not universally positive. The efficiency gain from punishment depends crucially on the punishment technology (cost and impact), the number of rounds (longer games allow efficiency to recover as punishment use declines), and the presence or absence of anti-social punishment. If punishment is low-cost and high-impact, and/or the game is long, efficiency is likely to increase. If punishment is costly relative to its impact, or if anti-social punishment is prevalent, efficiency may decrease even as cooperation rises. Control efficiency (without punishment) is a necessary baseline, but the effect of punishment is highly sensitive to these design dimensions. The survey also highlights that communication, sorting, and non-monetary sanctions can sometimes achieve similar or better efficiency gains than monetary punishment.

- source: Hilbe, C., Traulsen, A., Röhl, T., & Milinski, M. (2014). Democratic decisions establish stable authorities that overcome the paradox of second-order punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that second-order punishment institutions (which punish both defectors and those who do not support the punishment institution) result in higher average payoffs and cooperation rates than institutions without second-order punishment. When given a choice, groups using majority voting overwhelmingly select the more efficient second-order punishment institution, while groups using individual migration (foot voting) initially avoid it, resulting in lower payoffs. Over time, some foot-voting groups shift toward the more efficient institution, but not as consistently as with majority voting. The findings highlight the importance of democratic decision-making in establishing efficient punishment institutions that sustain cooperation and maximize group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling second-order punishment in a public goods game increases efficiency (average payoff) compared to a control without such punishment, under otherwise identical game parameters. The effect is robust across both theoretical predictions and experimental results. The findings also show that the method of institutional choice (majority voting vs. individual migration) moderates the realized efficiency, with majority voting more reliably leading to the efficient outcome. For prediction tasks, the presence of second-order punishment, the method of institutional choice, and the group’s prior experience with institutional regimes are important moderators. The paper’s detailed reporting of payoffs under both control and treatment conditions makes it directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Dannenberg, A., & Gallier, C. (2020). The choice of institutions to solve cooperation problems: a survey of experimental research. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The review finds that (1) punishment institutions, when adopted, reliably increase both cooperation and group efficiency/payoff in public goods and related games; (2) the effect is strongest when the institution eliminates free-riding incentives and is not too costly; (3) the popularity and effectiveness of punishment institutions increase with repeated choice and learning; (4) endogenously chosen punishment institutions often yield higher efficiency than exogenously imposed ones, especially for weaker institutions, but the aggregate benefit of endogenous choice is ambiguous because some groups fail to implement the institution; (5) institutional cost and incomplete elimination of free-riding are key barriers to adoption and effectiveness; (6) most studies report both behavioral and payoff outcomes, with efficiency and group payoff being central dependent variables.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides strong evidence that, in public goods game-like environments, enabling punishment mechanisms generally increases group efficiency and payoffs, especially when the punishment is effective (eliminates free-riding) and not too costly. The effect is robust across a variety of game designs, player counts, and institutional choice mechanisms. For prediction, the presence of a punishment institution should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, with the magnitude moderated by punishment cost, effectiveness, and whether the institution is adopted. The review also highlights that repeated exposure, information, and learning opportunities increase the likelihood of institution adoption and thus efficiency gains. However, if the institution is costly or does not fully deter free-riding, efficiency gains may be limited or absent. The review's synthesis of many studies with detailed design features makes it highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Kraft-Todd, G. T., Rand, D. G., Nowak, M. A., & Norton, M. (2021). Invisible inequality leads to punishing the poor and rewarding the rich. *Behavioural Public Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated public goods games with unequal endowments, allowing punishment and/or reward, the visibility of income distribution has a strong effect on sanctioning and contribution behavior. When incomes are hidden, participants tend to punish the poor (for low absolute contributions) and reward the rich (for high absolute contributions). When incomes are revealed, this pattern reverses: the poor are rewarded (for high percentage contributions) and the rich are punished (for low percentage contributions). Revealing incomes increases total contributions to the public good and reduces final payoff inequality (lower Gini coefficient). The effect is robust whether incomes are assigned randomly or earned via performance. The paper does not report group efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but the increase in total contributions and reduction in inequality suggest a more positive effect on efficiency when income is revealed and punishment/reward is available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with unequal endowments and punishment/reward options, making income information visible increases total contributions and reduces payoff inequality, especially by shifting sanctioning away from the poor and toward the rich. For prediction tasks, the presence of income transparency (showing others' endowments) is a key moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency-related outcomes. The game design dimensions most directly supported are player count (5), number of rounds (10), presence of punishment and reward, unequal endowments, and the manipulation of information about others' incomes. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a context where income is visible will have a more positive effect on efficiency and equality than when income is hidden. However, the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, so the exact magnitude of the effect is not available.

- source: Chugunova, M., Luhan, W. J., & Nicklisch, A. (2020). When to leave carrots for sticks: On the evolution of sanctioning institutions in open communities. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Participants initially choose reward-based public goods communities, which provide higher payoffs and are preferred by most. Over time, as rewards decline and inequality increases in the reward community, some participants migrate to the punishment community. The punishment community achieves higher contribution rates, but lower average payoffs compared to the reward community. Importantly, when punishment and reward communities compete, the punishment community requires less actual punishment to sustain cooperation, reducing welfare losses typically associated with punishment. The threat of punishment, rather than its frequent use, is sufficient to maintain high contributions. However, the reward community still retains a majority of participants and maintains higher average payoffs, though the difference with the punishment community narrows over time.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the institutional context. In isolation, punishment communities sustain high contributions but at a welfare cost due to punishment expenditures. When a reward community is available, migration dynamics mean that the punishment community can sustain cooperation with less actual punishment, reducing welfare losses. Thus, the negative efficiency impact of punishment is mitigated when there is institutional competition. For prediction, this implies that the efficiency of punishment-enabled games may be higher when players can choose between institutions, and that the mere threat of punishment can be effective if migration is possible. However, in direct comparison, reward communities yield higher payoffs than punishment communities, though the gap narrows over time.

- source: Hou, G. L., Wang, F., Shi, J. Y., Chen, W. J., & Yu, J. (2019). Which is the ideal sanction for cooperation? An experimental study on different types of third-party sanctions. *Psych Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third-party punishment and the combination of third-party punishment and reward both significantly increase the average level of cooperation in a public goods game compared to a baseline with no sanctions. Third-party reward alone has little effect on cooperation. The combination of punishment and reward (TP/R) leads to the highest average cooperation, though the difference with punishment alone is not statistically significant. Both punishment and TP/R treatments maintain high cooperation throughout the game, while cooperation declines in the baseline and reward-only treatments. In terms of payoffs, third-party interventions (especially punishment and TP/R) change the earnings of PGG-players, generally improving the alignment between contribution and payoff. The paper provides evidence that third-party sanctions, especially those involving punishment, can increase group earnings and improve the outcome of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment in a standard PGG increases group earnings and cooperation compared to a baseline with no sanctions. The effect is even more pronounced when both punishment and reward are available, suggesting a complementary effect. The game design dimensions (e.g., player count, number of rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech) are clearly specified, making the results directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in similar lab PGGs. The findings support the prediction that, holding control efficiency constant, enabling punishment (especially with a 3:1 punishment tech and moderate cost) will increase efficiency. Reward alone is much less effective. The results are most applicable to lab PGGs with 4 players, 10 rounds, no chat, and full information about others' contributions and payoffs.

- source: Fehr, E., & Gächter, S. (2002). Altruistic punishment in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with random group assignment and no reputation or repeated interaction, the opportunity for costly punishment (1 MU cost to punisher, 3 MU cost to target per point) leads to a large and sustained increase in average contributions compared to a no-punishment baseline. Most punishment is directed at below-average contributors by above-average contributors. The threat and execution of punishment both increase cooperation. Individual income is maximized when contributions are close to the group average, and both positive and negative deviations reduce income. The emotional mechanism underlying punishment is negative affect toward free riders. The paper does not report group efficiency as a percentage of the social optimum, but the data and payoff structure imply that efficiency is much higher with punishment enabled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in a standard linear public goods game with four players, six rounds, no communication, continuous contribution choice, and no reputation or repeated interaction. The control (no-punishment) condition shows declining contributions and low efficiency, while the punishment condition shows increasing contributions and much higher efficiency. The punishment mechanism is costly (1:3 cost:impact), and punishment is anonymous. The findings support the prediction that, in similar designs, enabling punishment will substantially increase group efficiency relative to the control. The paper does not manipulate other design dimensions, so its predictive value is strongest for games matching its structure.

- source: Wang, C. Q., & Huang, C. C. (2022). Between local and global strategy updating in public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Introducing the option for costly punishment in a repeated public goods game increases group contributions and group payoffs compared to the no-punishment baseline. This effect holds both when sanctions are observable (feedback given immediately) and when sanctions are unobservable (feedback delayed until the end). The mechanism is that subjects expect free riders to be punished and adjust their behavior to avoid being punished, even when they do not observe sanctions in real time. Sanctioning is mainly non-strategic and norm-driven, targeting free riders. The presence of punishment stabilizes cooperation and prevents the unraveling of contributions, with some groups achieving high cooperation and others not. The overall effect of punishment on efficiency (as measured by group payoffs and contributions as a fraction of maximum) is positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling costly punishment in a standard linear public goods game increases group efficiency (payoff) compared to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to whether punishment is observable or not during play, suggesting that the mere possibility of punishment, and the associated social norm, is sufficient to increase cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that adding punishment to a repeated linear PGG with 4 players, 10 rounds, and MPCR=0.4 will increase efficiency, even if punishment is not immediately observable. The magnitude of the effect may vary by group, but the direction is reliably positive. The paper also provides parameter values for key design dimensions.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a public goods game with 4-person groups and 10 rounds, adding a costly punishment stage (1 token cost, 3 token impact) significantly increases average contributions compared to a no-punishment baseline. However, average group earnings are lower in the punishment condition (19.4 tokens) than in the no-punishment condition (23.6 tokens), indicating that the efficiency cost of punishment outweighs the gains from increased cooperation. The study also finds that initial group contribution heterogeneity strongly predicts long-run outcomes, and that the between-subjects design (no prior exposure to the no-punishment game) yields higher contributions than the within-subjects design used in previous studies. The findings generalize to other cities with similar socio-economic profiles.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard linear public goods game with 4 players, 10 rounds, and a punishment mechanism (cost 1, impact 3, anonymous, no chat, no rewards), enabling punishment increases average contributions but reduces group efficiency/payoff compared to the no-punishment baseline. The control (no-punishment) efficiency is higher than the treatment (punishment) efficiency, due to the costliness of sanctions. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, no rewards, anonymous punishment) are all clearly specified, making this study highly informative for similar settings. The results suggest that, unless punishment is very efficient or antisocial punishment is rare, introducing costly punishment may not improve group efficiency even if it raises cooperation.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Malaeb, B., Vlassopoulos, M., & Wahba, J. (2021). Cooperation in a fragmented society: Experimental evidence on Syrian refugees and natives in Lebanon. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that: (1) Homogeneous groups (same nationality) contribute more to the public good than mixed groups, and this is mainly due to Lebanese participants reducing contributions in mixed groups. (2) Punishment is used more in homogeneous groups, and there is substantial antisocial punishment, especially among Lebanese. (3) The opportunity to punish does not increase contributions overall; in fact, average contributions decrease in the punishment stage for Syrians and do not change significantly for Lebanese or mixed groups. (4) Average earnings after the contribution stage are higher in homogeneous groups, but total earnings (including punishment costs) are not significantly different between treatments, as mixed groups assign less punishment and thus incur lower punishment costs. (5) The presence of punishment does not remedy the lower cooperation in mixed groups. (6) Behavior in mixed groups is more conditionally cooperative, while homogeneous groups show more unconditional cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of punishment in a two-player repeated linear public goods game with a 0.75 MPCR and a 1:3 punishment cost-to-impact ratio. The main finding is that the introduction of punishment does not increase efficiency or average earnings; in fact, contributions do not rise and may even fall, especially due to antisocial punishment. While punishment increases costs (especially in homogeneous groups), it does not lead to higher total payoffs. Thus, for similar game designs (small groups, moderate MPCR, punishment with 1:3 cost-impact, no communication), one should not expect punishment to increase efficiency, especially in settings with potential for antisocial punishment. The paper also highlights that group composition (in-group vs. out-group) can moderate both cooperation and punishment, but the overall effect of punishment on efficiency is neutral or negative in this context.

- source: Fehl, K., Sommerfeld, R. D., Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Milinski, M. (2012). I Dare You to Punish Me-Vendettas in Games of Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In public goods games with multiple rounds of punishment, participants often retaliate and escalate into vendettas, incurring high costs. Although cooperation increases, the costs of punishment and counter-punishment reduce overall earnings, especially for those involved in vendettas. The negative impact on payoffs is clear: participants involved in vendettas or retaliation earn significantly less than those who abstain. In contrast, in dyadic games, punishment and vendettas are rare, and most participants act cooperatively, so efficiency losses from punishment are minimal. The study highlights that while punishment can increase cooperation, it can also lead to costly vendettas that undermine group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling repeated, peer-based punishment in public goods games can increase cooperation but at the cost of reduced efficiency due to costly vendettas. The negative effect on payoffs is especially pronounced for those who engage in cycles of retaliation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, even if control efficiency is moderate, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency and can even reduce it if vendettas are likely. The design dimensions most relevant here are the number of punishment rounds, the ability to identify punishers, and the group vs. dyad structure. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with repeated, open punishment opportunities and identifiable punishers.

- source: Harrell, A., & Wolff, T. (2023). Cooperation in Networked Collective-Action Groups: Information Access and Norm Enforcement in Groups of Different Sizes. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This experiment demonstrates that punishment opportunities in public goods games (PGGs) increase cooperation and, over time, group earnings, especially in large and densely connected groups. In information-only conditions (no punishment), dense networks increase cooperation in small groups but not in large groups, where dense information can actually decrease cooperation. When punishment is enabled, dense networks increase cooperation in both small and large groups, but the effect is especially strong in large groups. Although punishment is initially costly and reduces earnings, the resulting increase in cooperation leads to higher group profits in later rounds. The study provides direct evidence that the efficiency gains from punishment depend on both group size and network density.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in PGGs increases group efficiency (as measured by group earnings/profits) over time, particularly in large and densely connected groups. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment should be expected to have a more positive effect on efficiency in large groups with dense networks than in small or sparsely connected groups. The effect is less pronounced or even negative in large groups without punishment, and in small groups, dense information alone can suffice to sustain cooperation. The findings suggest that the interaction between group size, network density, and punishment availability is critical for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Herrmann, B., Thöni, C., & Gächter, S. (2008). Antisocial punishment across societies. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a punishment option in a repeated public goods game increases cooperation and group earnings in some societies, but not in others. In societies with high levels of antisocial punishment, the positive effect of punishment on cooperation and group earnings is diminished or absent. The variation in outcomes is strongly associated with societal-level factors such as norms of civic cooperation and the strength of the rule of law. The study demonstrates that punishment is not universally beneficial for efficiency or cooperation; its effect depends on the prevalence of antisocial punishment, which varies widely across societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly context-dependent. When predicting the efficiency of a game with punishment enabled, it is crucial to consider not only the game design dimensions but also the likelihood of antisocial punishment, which may be influenced by societal or group-level norms. In environments similar to those with low antisocial punishment (e.g., Western Europe, US), punishment is likely to increase efficiency. In environments with high antisocial punishment, punishment may have little or even negative effect on efficiency. The game design dimensions in this study (e.g., player count, rounds, punishment cost/tech, anonymity) are held constant, so the main moderator is the social context, not the game mechanics. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment) unless antisocial punishment is low.

- source: Eisenberg, T., & Engel, C. (2014). Assuring Civil Damages Adequately Deter: A Public Good Experiment. *Journal of Empirical Legal Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that damages rules analogous to civil litigation (compensatory, class action, treble damages) can deter free riding and promote cooperation in a repeated public good game. The baseline (no punishment) shows rapid decay of contributions and group payoffs. Compensatory damages (limited to individual harm) slow but do not prevent decay. Class action damages (based on group harm) and treble damages, when sufficiently likely or severe, stabilize contributions and maintain higher group payoffs. The effect is not due to the theoretical threat of punishment, but to participants' actual experience of being punished. The option for the punisher to forfeit rather than retain damages (i.e., to punish without personal gain) is rarely used and does not affect outcomes. Thus, the deterrent effect of damages is robust to whether the punisher benefits personally. The findings suggest that for punishment to sustain cooperation and group welfare, it must be sufficiently severe and/or likely, and that actual experience with sanctions is more important than their mere possibility.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (damages) in a repeated public good game increases group efficiency (payoff) relative to the no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is severe (treble damages) or based on group harm (class action) and is likely to be imposed. The effect is not simply on average contribution, but on the stability of cooperation and group payoffs over time. For prediction tasks, the presence and design of punishment (severity, scope, probability) are key moderators of efficiency gains. Control efficiency (baseline group payoff) is improved by adding punishment, but the magnitude depends on the punishment regime. The paper also shows that the actual experience of being punished is a stronger driver than the theoretical threat, suggesting that models should account for realized, not just expected, punishment. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially for settings with repeated interaction, group feedback, and endogenous punishment.

- source: Tan, F. F. (2008). Punishment in a Linear Public Good Game with Productivity Heterogeneity. *Economist-netherlands*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a four-person linear public good game, groups with heterogeneous productivity (MPCRs 0.9 and 0.3, average 0.6) contribute less than homogeneous groups (all MPCR 0.6) when punishment is not allowed. Introducing a costly punishment mechanism (punisher pays 1, target loses 2 per point) in heterogeneous groups significantly increases average contributions to levels similar to homogeneous groups without punishment. However, the cost of punishment means that average group earnings (welfare) are not higher with punishment: over 15 rounds, average earnings are similar across all treatments, and in early periods, punishment reduces earnings. In the final periods, punishment helps maintain earnings by countering free-riding. Punishment is mainly assigned by high-productivity agents to low-productivity agents in successful groups. High-productivity agents are more responsive to punishment when their contributions are below average.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a linear PGG with productivity heterogeneity, enabling costly punishment increases average contributions but does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff, due to the cost of punishment. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to heterogeneity, enabling punishment will likely increase contributions, but the net efficiency gain may be small or absent unless punishment is cheap or highly effective. The effect of punishment on efficiency is time-dependent: in later rounds, punishment can prevent the collapse of cooperation and thus improve earnings relative to no-punishment, but over the whole game, the cost of punishment can offset these gains. The findings are most relevant for games with 4 players, 15 rounds, no communication, continuous contributions, and a punishment technology of 1:2 cost:impact.

- source: Chen, J. C., D'Attoma, J., Fonseca, M. A., & Malezieux, A. (2025). Promoting Cooperation in an Unequal World: Experimental Evidence on the Role of Transparency and Punishment. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two lab experiments using a four-player linear public goods game with unequal endowments (determined by merit and luck), the paper finds that: (1) Peer punishment increases contributions and group payoffs, but only when endowments are transparent. (2) When endowments are secret, punishment has a limited effect on cooperation and does not reduce inequality; advantaged participants contribute a lower fraction of their endowment and earn much higher payoffs. (3) When endowments are made public, punishment becomes much more effective at increasing contributions and reducing inequality, especially among the advantaged. (4) Willingness to pay for secrecy is higher among advantaged participants, especially when their advantage is due to luck. (5) Overall, transparency is necessary for punishment to improve efficiency and mitigate the negative effects of inequality.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly dependent on whether endowments (wealth) are transparent. In games with secret endowments, punishment has little effect on group payoff or efficiency; in games with public endowments, punishment significantly increases contributions and group payoffs, especially among the advantaged. For prediction, the presence of transparency (showing endowments or relevant summaries) is a key moderator: punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when transparency is high. The game uses continuous contributions, no chat, and a fixed MPCR of 0.4. Punishment is costly (cost 1, impact 3), and there is no reward option. The findings are most directly applicable to games with similar design features.

- source: Dal Bó, E., & Dal Bó, P. (2014). Do the right thing: The effects of moral suasion on cooperation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a series of lab experiments using a two-player linear public goods game, the authors show that (1) moral suasion (exposure to a moral message) increases contributions and group payoffs, but the effect is short-lived when punishment is not available; (2) when a punishment stage is added (where players can pay to reduce their partner's payoff), the effect of moral suasion becomes persistent, with higher and sustained contributions and higher group payoffs; (3) punishment alone increases contributions compared to no-punishment, but does not prevent a decline over time; (4) the combination of punishment and moral suasion produces the highest and most persistent cooperation and payoffs; (5) the mechanisms include both changes in expectations about others and changes in preferences. The paper does not report efficiency as a ratio, but does report group payoffs and their changes, which are closely related.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player linear public goods game with random rematching, adding a punishment stage (with a cost to the punisher and a larger cost to the punished) increases group payoffs and sustains higher cooperation, especially when combined with moral suasion. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive, and the effect is amplified when moral suasion is present. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contributions, etc.) are well specified, making the results directly relevant for predicting efficiency in similar lab PGGs with and without punishment. The paper is less informative about settings with more players, field contexts, or different punishment technologies, but is highly informative for standard lab PGGs.

- source: DeAngelo, G., & Gee, L. K. (2020). Peers or police?: The effect of choice and type of monitoring in the provision of public goods. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment uses a 2x2 design to compare exogenous vs. endogenous monitoring and peer vs. group monitoring in a linear public goods game with punishment. When monitoring is exogenously imposed, both peer and group monitoring yield similar public good provision and net earnings. When monitoring is endogenously chosen, group monitoring collapses (due to free riders refusing to pay for monitoring), leading to a 44% drop in public good provision, but net earnings are not significantly lower because less is spent on punishment. Peer monitoring, however, is robust to endogenization: public good provision and net earnings remain high, and willingness-to-pay for monitoring is not strongly correlated with contribution level. The results are robust to changes in the punishment mechanism and the voting rule for group monitoring. The key implication is that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency depends on the monitoring regime and whether monitoring is exogenous or endogenous.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the impact depends on the monitoring regime and whether monitoring is exogenously imposed or endogenously chosen. If monitoring is exogenous (automatic), punishment increases or maintains efficiency (via higher public good provision) regardless of whether monitoring is peer-based or group-based. If monitoring is endogenous and requires group consensus, punishment may fail to improve efficiency because monitoring is not implemented (especially in the presence of free riders), leading to low public good provision but not necessarily lower net earnings due to reduced punishment costs. Peer monitoring is robust to endogenization: punishment maintains efficiency even when monitoring is endogenous. Thus, when using game design dimensions to predict treatment efficiency, it is crucial to account for whether monitoring is exogenous or endogenous and the type of monitoring (peer vs. group). Control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the paper provides sufficient data to estimate the relative effect of punishment under different monitoring regimes.

- source: Barclay, P., & Raihani, N. (2016). Partner choice versus punishment in human Prisoner's Dilemmas. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across four studies, the paper finds that (1) punishers are rated as more trustworthy and group focused than nonpunishers; (2) after repeated PGG rounds, punishers are trusted more and receive more money in trust games, but this effect is absent after a single round; (3) justified punishment (punishing free riders) leads to increased trust and monetary benefits, while unjustified punishment leads to reduced trust and earnings; (4) the reputational and monetary benefits of justified punishment can offset or even exceed the cost of punishing, especially in repeated interactions. The paper does not directly report group efficiency ratios, but does show that punishment can increase individual and group monetary outcomes via increased trust and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated PGGs with punishment, justified punishment can increase the monetary benefits to punishers via enhanced trust and subsequent transfers, partially or fully offsetting the cost of punishment. The effect is contingent on repeated interaction (number of rounds) and the justification for punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in repeated PGGs (with visible identities and reputation) is likely to increase group efficiency and individual payoffs, especially when punishment is justified and recognized as such. In single-round games, these benefits do not materialize. The paper does not provide direct group efficiency ratios, but the monetary outcomes and trust game results are closely related and informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Muthukrishna, M., Francois, P., Pourahmadi, S., & Henrich, J. (2017). Corrupting cooperation and how anti-corruption strategies may backfire. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that allowing bribery in an institutional punishment public goods game (IPGG) substantially reduces public good provisioning (by about 25%). In the standard IPGG (control), stronger leaders (higher punishment multiplier) increase contributions and public good provisioning. However, when bribery is possible (bribery game, BG), stronger leaders actually decrease public good provisioning, as they use their power to extract bribes rather than enforce cooperation. Anti-corruption strategies (partial/full transparency, leader investment) can increase contributions in some contexts (strong leaders or high economic potential), but may have no effect or even reduce contributions further in contexts with weak leaders and poor economic potential. The main outcome is mean contribution to the public good, which is a close proxy for efficiency. The results show that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent and can be reversed or undermined by corruption opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the presence of corruption opportunities (e.g., bribery) can reverse the usual positive effect of institutional punishment on efficiency. The effect of punishment depends critically on whether leaders can be corrupted, the strength of the leader's punitive power, and the economic potential (mpcr). In standard IPGGs, stronger punishment increases efficiency, but in bribery-enabled games, stronger punishment can decrease efficiency. Anti-corruption interventions (transparency, leader investment) can help, but only under certain structural conditions. When using this paper for prediction, it is important to consider whether the game design allows for corruption/bribery, the strength of punishment, and the economic context. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported directly, but the relative changes are clear: punishment increases efficiency in standard IPGG, but decreases it when bribery is possible.

- source: Dugar, S. (2013). NON-MONETARY INCENTIVES AND OPPORTUNISTIC BEHAVIOR: EVIDENCE FROM A LABORATORY PUBLIC GOOD GAME. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of four, the introduction of non-monetary, costless disapproval points (social sanctions) after each round leads to significantly higher group contributions and group earnings (efficiency) compared to a baseline with no such mechanism. Approval points (social rewards) alone have a small, weakly significant effect on efficiency. The combination of approval and disapproval points produces the highest efficiency, with group earnings and contributions both significantly higher than in any other treatment. The effect is robust over 15 rounds, though some decay is observed. The study demonstrates that even costless, payoff-irrelevant social sanctions can substantially improve group welfare in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a costless, non-monetary punishment mechanism (disapproval points) in a standard linear public goods game increases efficiency (group earnings) relative to a baseline with no such mechanism. The effect is larger than that of a costless reward mechanism (approval points), and the combination of both is most effective. Because the punishment is costless and does not reduce group payoffs, all efficiency gains are realized. For prediction tasks, this suggests that introducing a non-monetary, costless punishment mechanism in a repeated, anonymous, four-player, 15-round linear PGG with MPCR=0.4 will increase efficiency substantially over the control. The effect is likely to be even larger if both approval and disapproval are available. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with costless, anonymous, non-monetary sanctions, and may not generalize to costly or monetary punishment.

- source: Karakostas, A., Kocher, M. G., Matzat, D., Rau, H. A., & Riewe, G. (2023). The team allocator game: Allocation power in public goods games. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper experimentally compares standard public goods games (VCM) to versions with a team allocator (TAG) who can redistribute the public good among team members. Three production technologies are tested: linear, weakest-link, and best-shot. In the linear case, the team allocator increases both contributions and group efficiency (total profit), as the allocator uses allocation power to reward contributors and sanction free-riders without explicit punishment costs. In the best-shot case, the allocator increases contributions but causes over-contribution and reduces efficiency due to coordination problems. In the weakest-link case, the allocator has no significant effect on contributions or efficiency. Thus, the effect of hierarchical allocation power as an implicit sanction/reward mechanism depends crucially on the production technology.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of introducing a sanctioning/reward mechanism (via a team allocator) on efficiency depends on the public goods production function. In linear PGGs, such mechanisms can increase efficiency, but in best-shot games, they may reduce efficiency due to over-contribution. In weakest-link games, the effect is negligible. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the production technology (linear, best-shot, weakest-link), the presence of an allocator with discretionary redistribution power (as a proxy for punishment/reward), and the absence of explicit punishment costs. Control efficiency (VCM) is a strong predictor, but the treatment effect varies by technology. The findings suggest that the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms is positive in linear games, negative in best-shot, and neutral in weakest-link games, even when the mechanism is costless to the allocator.

- source: Cinyabuguma, M., Page, T., & Putterman, L. (2005). Cooperation under the threat of expulsion in a public goods experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compared a standard public goods game (VCM) with a treatment in which group members could vote to expel others. In the expulsion treatment, contributions rose to nearly 100% of endowments, and average earnings (efficiency) were significantly higher than in the baseline. Expulsion was used sparingly, but the threat alone was enough to sustain cooperation. Expelled members earned much less, but overall group efficiency was still higher with expulsion. When the expulsion threat was removed, cooperation collapsed. The results suggest that the threat of exclusion is a powerful mechanism for sustaining cooperation and improving efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a credible expulsion (ostracism) mechanism in a public goods game with known group size, fixed rounds, and full information about others' contributions can substantially increase efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust even when the punishment (expulsion) is rarely used, as the threat alone is sufficient. The design dimensions—large group size (16), 15 known rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, no reward option, and full feedback—are all specified, making the findings directly applicable to prediction tasks involving similar settings. The expulsion mechanism is a form of binary, irreversible punishment with a small cost to punishers. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling expulsion or strong exclusionary sanctions will lead to much higher efficiency than control.

- source: Maier-Rigaud, F. P., Martinsson, P., & Staffiero, G. (2010). Ostracism and the provision of a public good: experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that allowing group members to ostracize (exclude) others by majority vote in a repeated linear public goods game leads to a substantial and statistically significant increase in group contributions and average earnings (efficiency) compared to the standard game without ostracism. This effect is robust except in the first and last periods, where contributions are similar across treatments due to initial uncertainty and end-game effects. Ostracism is targeted at low contributors, and after initial exclusions, remaining members tend to converge to high contribution levels. The positive effect on efficiency is observed both when ostracism is introduced first or second in the session, and is higher than in the control. The design ensures that the only difference between treatments is the possibility of exclusion, and there is no direct monetary punishment or cost to voting. The results suggest that ostracism is an effective endogenous sanctioning mechanism for sustaining cooperation and increasing group welfare in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling ostracism (exclusion by majority vote) in a repeated linear public goods game with fixed partners, full information on others' contributions, and no cost to voting, leads to a substantial increase in group efficiency (average earnings as a fraction of the social optimum) compared to the same game without ostracism. The effect is robust across session orderings and is not offset by the reduction in group size due to exclusion. For prediction tasks, the presence of an ostracism mechanism (even with no direct monetary punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially in games with similar design parameters (6 players, 10 rounds, continuous contributions, MPCR=0.4, no chat, no reward, no punishment cost, anonymous feedback on contributions).
