The effect is strongest in the middle rounds and may diminish at the end due to end-game effects.

- source: Feinberg, M., Willer, R., & Schultz, M. (2014). Gossip and Ostracism Promote Cooperation in Groups. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that in a repeated public goods game with random rematching, the ability to gossip about others' contributions and to ostracize (exclude) group members based on this information leads to a substantial increase in cooperation and, after several rounds, to the highest group and individual earnings. Gossip alone also increases cooperation and earnings compared to a basic game with no reputation or exclusion, but the effect is less pronounced and decays over time. Ostracism is used primarily against low contributors, and those who are ostracized tend to increase their contributions upon returning. The efficiency gains from gossip and ostracism are not immediate, as exclusion temporarily reduces group size and the public good multiplier, but by the final rounds, these groups outperform the others in earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a punishment-like mechanism (ostracism) in addition to reputation/gossip in a public goods game increases efficiency (group and individual earnings) compared to both a basic game and a gossip-only game. The effect is robust despite a temporary efficiency loss due to exclusion. The design dimensions—such as group size (4), number of rounds (6 per game), no chat, continuous contributions, MPCR of 0.5 (or 0.375 when group size drops to 3), and anonymous exclusion—are all specified, making this study highly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment (ostracism) in similar lab PGGs. The results suggest that, even when punishment is implemented as exclusion rather than direct costly punishment, and even when exclusion temporarily reduces group size and multiplier, the long-run effect on efficiency is positive if reputation information is available.

- source: Masclet, D. (2003). Ostracism in work teams: a public good experiment. *International Journal of Manpower*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that allowing group members to exclude (ostracize) others after observing their contributions in a public good game leads to much higher contributions and significantly higher group payoffs compared to a control without exclusion. Both costless and costly exclusion treatments yield similar increases in efficiency, with group payoffs rising by about 28-30% relative to the control. Actual exclusions are rare, but the threat is sufficient to sustain cooperation. The effect is robust to whether exclusion is directly costly or not. The study provides direct evidence that non-monetary peer punishment (ostracism) can substantially improve efficiency in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling exclusion (ostracism) in a standard public good game with four players and continuous contributions leads to a large and robust increase in efficiency (group payoff), regardless of whether exclusion is directly costly or not. For prediction tasks, the presence of an exclusion mechanism (even if rarely used) should be expected to substantially increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, under similar game parameters (n=4, 10 rounds, MPCR=0.4, no chat, continuous contributions, no rewards). The effect is observed even when the control efficiency is low and is robust to the cost structure of exclusion. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment-like mechanisms (ostracism) in similar lab PGG designs.

- source: Güth, W., Levati, M. V., Sutter, M., & Van der Heijden, E. (2007). Leading by example with and without exclusion power in voluntary contribution experiments. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a linear public goods game with four players and 24 rounds, the introduction of a leader who moves first increases contributions and group profits compared to the standard simultaneous game. When the leader is given exclusion power (the ability to exclude a follower from the next round), both contributions and group profits rise substantially more. The effect of exclusion power is robust to whether the leader is fixed or rotates among group members. In the endogenous phase, only about 40% of groups successfully appoint a leader, but those that do achieve much higher contributions and profits than those that do not. The presence of exclusion power (punishment) is a strong moderator of the positive effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment via exclusion power in a public goods game with leadership substantially increases group efficiency compared to both the standard game and leadership without punishment. The effect is robust across fixed and rotating leader appointment. The control (no leader) game yields the lowest efficiency, leadership without punishment yields a moderate increase, and leadership with punishment yields the highest efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment (exclusion), the marginal per-capita return, and the group size are all well-specified and informative. The absence of communication, the use of continuous contribution, and the lack of reward options are also clearly specified. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in similar game designs.

- source: Fatas, E., Morales, A. J., & Ubeda, P. (2010). Blind justice: An experimental analysis of random punishment in team production. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implements a public goods game with a novel random punishment mechanism: after each round, each group member is excluded from the group benefit with probability 1-R, where R is the normalized group contribution. This 'blind' punishment does not target individual defectors. The experiment compares three conditions: standard VCM (no punishment), random punishment without redistribution (RP), and random punishment with redistribution (RPR). Results show that both RP and RPR significantly increase contributions compared to VCM. However, only RPR increases group earnings (efficiency), as RP's punishment cost outweighs the contribution gains. The effect of random punishment is similar whether introduced after a baseline or from the start. Behavioral analysis shows that high contributors reduce their contributions if punished, but overall, conditional cooperation persists and the net effect is positive for contributions. The mechanism does not fully prevent the decline in contributions over time.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that random, non-individually-targeted punishment can increase contributions in a standard public goods game, but its effect on efficiency depends on whether the penalty is redistributed. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism that is not individually targeted but is probabilistically linked to group performance will likely increase contributions, but may not increase efficiency unless the punishment cost is recycled within the group. The effect is robust to order and does not depend on communication or knowledge of individual contributions. The marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is 0.5, and the group size is 4. The findings suggest that, for similar designs, enabling random punishment will increase contributions and may increase efficiency if the punishment cost is not lost to the group.

- source: Dannenberg, A., Haita-Falah, C., & Zitzelsberger, S. (2020). Voting on the threat of exclusion in a public goods experiment. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of 5, the option to exclude (ostracize) members is introduced either costlessly (B10) or with a 20% endowment cost to all (B8). Groups can vote to implement the exclusion institution (endogenous) or have it imposed (exogenous). The exclusion institution leads to significantly higher contributions in all treatments. When exclusion is costless (B10), average payoffs (efficiency) are higher with exclusion than without. When exclusion is costly (B8), average payoffs are lower with exclusion than without, despite higher contributions. The exclusion mechanism is used almost exclusively to remove the lowest contributors, and the threat of exclusion increases cooperation among those who remain. The effect of exclusion on efficiency is positive when costless, but negative or neutral when costly. Endogenous and exogenous implementation yield similar results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment (here, exclusion/ostracism) on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the costliness of the punishment institution. When exclusion is costless, enabling punishment increases both contributions and efficiency. When exclusion is costly (reducing endowments), enabling punishment increases contributions but does not improve efficiency and may reduce it. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR), group size, and the presence/absence of communication are all specified, allowing for direct mapping to prediction tasks. The results are robust to endogenous vs exogenous institution choice. For prediction, the key moderator is whether the punishment institution imposes a cost on all group members; if so, efficiency gains are not realized even if cooperation rises.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with and without the possibility of exclusion, and with/without a central network position, the ability to exclude group members increases average group earnings (efficiency), though the effect is not always statistically significant. Central players contribute less than periphery players, but this does not reduce group efficiency. The presence of exclusion leads to higher contributions and earnings overall, and centrality increases inequality but not efficiency loss.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling exclusion (a form of sanction) in a standard linear public goods game increases group efficiency, even when network structure introduces heterogeneity (centrality). The effect of exclusion is positive on efficiency, and the presence of a central player does not reduce efficiency. For prediction, this suggests that adding exclusion/voting-based sanctions to a standard PGG with known control efficiency will likely increase efficiency, and that network centrality does not undermine this effect. The exclusion mechanism here is costless to the voter, but exclusion itself is costly to the excluded and can fragment the group. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with 5 players, 5 rounds per block, MPCR=0.6, no chat, continuous contributions, and full information about others' contributions.

- source: Riehm, T., Fugger, N., Gillen, P., Gretschko, V., & Werner, P. (2022). Social norms, sanctions, and conditional entry in markets with externalities: Evidence from an artefactual field experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that all-can-win intergroup competition (where multiple groups can be ranked first and avoid penalties) leads to large increases in cooperation and group output across all treatments. The effect is strongest when penalties for not being ranked first are sharp (T1, T2), supporting near-perfect cooperation and benefiting a majority of individuals, though not all. In these cases, average earnings are about the same with and without competition, as severe penalties for a few groups offset gains for the majority. In the weaker-penalty treatment (T3), cooperation increases less, and the effect on earnings is neutral. The results suggest that the design of the penalty function (i.e., the sharpness of incentives) is crucial for both cooperation and efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a rank-based, all-can-win intergroup competition mechanism in a standard linear public goods game can substantially increase cooperation and group output, especially when penalties for low-ranked groups are sharp. However, the effect on efficiency (average earnings) is not always positive: while most individuals benefit, severe penalties for a minority can offset group-level gains. For prediction, the sharpness of the penalty function (punishment tech) and the degree of coordination among groups are key moderators. If the control game (no competition) has low efficiency, introducing sharp all-can-win competition is likely to increase group output and may increase average earnings, but the risk of severe losses for some groups must be considered. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with intergroup competition as a sanctioning mechanism.

- source: Lippert, S., & Tremewan, J. (2021). Pledge-and-review in the laboratory. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares a standard two-player public goods game (PG) to three pledge-and-review (P&R) variants: fixed disagreement payoffs (F), heterogeneous/certainty (C), and uncertainty (U). In all P&R treatments, after pledges are made, both players must vote to accept the pledges for them to be implemented; otherwise, both receive a disagreement payoff. Efficiency (average payoffs as a fraction of the full cooperation benchmark) is significantly higher in all P&R treatments than in the control PG game. The efficiency gain is persistent in the fixed disagreement payoff treatment, with no decline over 20 rounds, while some decline is observed in the other P&R treatments. There is no significant difference in efficiency between the three P&R variants. The mechanism allows conditional cooperators to avoid the typical decline in cooperation seen in repeated public goods games. The P&R institution functions as a form of second-party punishment (via voting down low pledges), but with endogenous costs and without explicit costly punishment tokens.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that adding a pledge-and-review (P&R) mechanism—where players can veto low contributions after seeing pledges—substantially increases efficiency in a two-player, 20-round public goods game with continuous contributions and no communication. The effect is robust to variations in disagreement payoffs (fixed, heterogeneous, uncertain). For prediction, if the control (PG) efficiency is known, enabling a P&R-style review stage is likely to increase efficiency by about 10% of the full cooperation benchmark, with the effect persisting over repeated rounds (at least in the fixed disagreement payoff case). The P&R mechanism is not identical to standard costly punishment (no explicit punishment tokens), but functions similarly by allowing players to block low contributions at a cost. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, continuous-contribution games without chat, with full feedback, and a review/veto stage after pledges.

- source: Nhim, T., Schuch, E., & Richter, A. (2023). Water scarcity and support for costly institutions in public goods: Experimental evidence from Cambodia. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that costly institutions (minimum contribution/tax and strict enforcement with punishment) are broadly supported by participants, especially those with prior experience of water scarcity. Both types of costly institutions increase average contributions to the public good compared to costless regimes. However, only the minimum contribution (tax) regime leads to higher payoffs and welfare, while strict enforcement with costly punishment increases contributions but does not improve payoffs due to the cost of enforcement. The efficiency of the group (measured as payoff relative to the social optimum) is thus higher under the tax regime but lower under strict enforcement compared to their respective costless counterparts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of introducing costly punishment or enforcement institutions in public goods games depends on the specific design. Minimum contribution (tax) regimes can increase both contributions and efficiency (payoff), while strict enforcement with costly punishment increases contributions but can reduce efficiency due to the cost of enforcement. For prediction, the presence of a costly punishment mechanism does not guarantee higher efficiency; the cost and structure of the institution are critical. Control efficiency (payoff) and the specific institutional design (tax/minimum vs. costly strict punishment) must be considered. The findings are highly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially regarding the cost and type of punishment or enforcement.

- source: Eichenseer, M. (2023). Leading-by-example in public goods experiments: What do we know?. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper synthesizes 62 experimental studies (mainly linear public goods games) and conducts meta-analyses and weighted least squares regressions. It finds that sequential play (leadership) increases group efficiency compared to simultaneous play. Centralized punishment and reward (where only the leader can punish or reward) further increase efficiency, with punishment having a stronger effect than reward. Peer punishment/reward (where all can punish/reward) have even larger effects. Rotating leadership reduces efficiency. The marginal per capita return (MPCR) and group size also positively affect efficiency, but to a lesser extent. Leaders contribute more than followers, and followers contribute about 79% as much as leaders. Inequality within groups does not generally increase with leadership. Leaders' payoffs are similar to those of average players in simultaneous games. The paper provides quantitative estimates of effect sizes for each moderator.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, quantitative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment (and reward) on efficiency in public goods games, conditional on game design dimensions. The meta-regressions include variables for group size, number of rounds, MPCR, presence and type of punishment/reward, and leadership structure. The results show that adding centralized punishment (by a leader) increases efficiency by about 0.26 (on a 0-1 scale of contributions relative to optimum), and peer punishment increases it by about 0.47. The effect of reward is positive but smaller. Rotating leadership reduces efficiency. These findings can be used to adjust predicted efficiency when punishment is enabled, given the control efficiency and game design. The paper is highly relevant for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Hugh-Jones, D., & Perroni, C. (2017). The logic of costly punishment reversed: Expropriation of free-riders and outsiders. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the threat of group expropriation (a form of punishment) can increase public goods contributions, but only when the expropriation mechanism is sufficiently difficult to coordinate (i.e., requires a large group to act together). When expropriation is too easy (requires only one or two people), it is less targeted at free-riders, more arbitrary, and leads to lower contributions and more frequent expropriation, reducing efficiency. In the baseline (punishment enabled, M=3), contributions are significantly higher than in the control (no expropriation), and expropriation is mostly targeted at unique lowest contributors. When the minimum group size for expropriation is reduced (M=2 or M=1), expropriation increases and contributions fall. In heterogeneous groups, minorities are more likely to be targeted in some treatments, especially when ambiguity or a history of targeting exists, and may respond less to punishment. However, in the main treatments, overall group efficiency is not significantly different between homogeneous and heterogeneous groups. The findings suggest that the design of the punishment mechanism (especially the ease of coordination required for punishment) is a critical moderator of its effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends on the punishment technology, specifically the minimum group size required for successful punishment (expropriation). When punishment is difficult to coordinate (higher M), it is more likely to be used as a credible threat against free-riders, increasing contributions and efficiency. When punishment is too easy (low M), it is used more arbitrarily, contributions fall, and efficiency is reduced. For prediction, the dimension 'punishment_tech' (minimum group size for punishment, or ease of expropriation) is a key moderator: higher coordination requirements for punishment (higher M) are associated with more positive effects of punishment on efficiency, while lower requirements (lower M) can make punishment detrimental. Control efficiency (without punishment) is much lower than with well-targeted, hard-to-coordinate punishment. Heterogeneity (minority status) can moderate targeting and behavioral response, but does not robustly reduce efficiency unless ambiguity or a history of targeting is present. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially punishment_tech, and provide indirect evidence for the importance of targeting and coordination in punishment mechanisms.

- source: Del Ponte, A., Masiliunas, A., & Lim, N. (2025). Decentralized voluntary agreements do not reduce emissions in a climate change experiment. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: In a lab experiment with a public bads game (3 players, 18 rounds), the introduction of voluntary pledge-and-review agreements, automatic financial penalties for exceeding pledges, and/or peer evaluation (reputation contest) did not reduce average production or disaster risk compared to baseline. Most participants joined agreements and complied with their pledges, but pledges were not ambitious enough to reduce emissions. Financial penalties increased compliance but did not improve group efficiency, as participants could avoid penalties by not joining or by making unambitious pledges. Peer evaluation did not reduce production and even reduced the ambition of pledges. The results suggest that, in decentralized and voluntary settings, punishment mechanisms do not improve efficiency unless participation and ambition can be enforced.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a voluntary, decentralized public bads game with 3 players and 18 rounds, adding automatic financial penalties for exceeding pledges does not improve group efficiency or reduce emissions compared to baseline, because participants can avoid penalties by not joining or by making unambitious pledges. Peer evaluation (reputation-based non-monetary punishment) also does not improve efficiency and may reduce ambition. For prediction, this suggests that in similar voluntary, opt-in, pledge-based games, the presence of punishment mechanisms (even if automatic and with high penalty rates) will not increase efficiency unless there are mechanisms to ensure ambitious pledges and broad participation. Control efficiency is a good predictor of treatment efficiency when punishment is voluntary and avoidable.

- source: Heine, F., & Strobel, M. (2020). Reward and punishment in a team contest. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares reward and punishment mechanisms in a team contest game (with and without between-group competition). Groups of four play 15 rounds, deciding how much to contribute to a group lottery (contest) and then can reward or punish teammates at a cost. The main findings are: (1) Both punishment and reward mechanisms lead to over-contribution relative to Nash equilibrium, causing significant efficiency losses (rent dissipation). (2) Punishment does not increase efficiency; it reduces it, as punishing is costly and does not significantly increase contributions. (3) Rewarding increases contributions more than punishment, but is welfare-neutral (cost to giver equals benefit to receiver). (4) The contest environment (with an opponent group) leads to even greater inefficiency and lower average payoffs than the non-contest environment. (5) The R&P treatment (both reward and punishment available) in the contest environment is the least efficient, with the highest overspending and lowest mean income. (6) Receiving reward increases subsequent contributions, but punishment does not. (7) Overall, the presence of punishment does not solve the efficiency problem in this environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a team contest environment with public-good-like features, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency and may worsen it, especially when both reward and punishment are available. The effect of punishment is less positive (more negative) on efficiency compared to reward or baseline. The contest structure (between-group competition) amplifies inefficiency. For prediction, if the control game (no punishment) is already inefficient due to over-contribution, enabling punishment is likely to further reduce efficiency, not improve it. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, known MPCR, punishment cost and tech, no identification of punishers, no communication) are all specified and match standard lab contest/PGG designs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled in similar environments.

- source: Lierl, M. (2016). Social sanctions and informal accountability: Evidence from a laboratory experiment. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment studies a public goods game with two beneficiaries and one provider, where the provider can embezzle contributions. The main manipulation is the structure of costly, mutual sanctioning (punishment and reward) relationships: (1) no sanctioning, (2) only among beneficiaries, (3) between provider and one or both beneficiaries, and (4) combinations thereof. The key finding is that mutual sanctioning among beneficiaries increases public goods provision and efficiency compared to no-sanctioning control, but this effect disappears or reverses when the provider is also part of the sanctioning network. The highest efficiency is observed when only beneficiaries can sanction each other. When both beneficiaries and the provider can mutually sanction, efficiency is not improved and can be lower than control. Actual punishment/reward is rare; the effect is mainly anticipatory. The results challenge the idea that denser or more encompassing sanctioning networks always improve efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the network structure of sanctioning relationships. If only beneficiaries can punish each other, enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to control. If the provider can also punish or be punished, the effect is reduced or negative. For prediction, the presence and direction of punishment effects should be conditioned on who can punish whom. The game is a close variant of a standard PGG, with the addition of a third-party provider who can embezzle, so results are most directly applicable to co-production or agency settings. Control efficiency (no-sanction) is reported, and treatment efficiency (with punishment) varies by network structure. The findings suggest that simply enabling punishment is not always efficiency-improving; the details of the punishment network matter.

- source: Colombier, N., Masclet, D., Mirza, D., & Montmarquette, C. (2011). GLOBAL SECURITY POLICIES AGAINST TERRORISM AND THE FREE RIDING PROBLEM: AN EXPERIMENTAL APPROACH. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment simulating international counterterrorism cooperation as a public good with a stochastic loss, the authors find that, absent institutional incentives, most participants defect and invest in private protection rather than the collective good. Introducing punishment (sanction) or reward institutions significantly increases investment in the collective account. However, while both punishment and reward reduce the probability of a loss event and increase first-stage payoffs, only reward institutions lead to higher overall welfare. Punishment increases cooperation but the direct costs of punishing (borne by both punishers and targets) more than offset the efficiency gains from increased cooperation, resulting in lower final payoffs than the baseline. Reward institutions, by contrast, increase both cooperation and final payoffs. Thus, punishment is not efficiency-enhancing in this environment, while reward is.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with costly punishment, enabling punishment increases cooperation but reduces overall efficiency compared to a no-punishment baseline. The costs of punishment (to both punisher and target) outweigh the benefits from increased cooperation. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should expect a less positive or even negative effect on group payoff/welfare, especially when punishment is costly and the structure is similar to this experiment (n=4, 40 rounds, costly punishment, no identification of punishers, no chat, continuous contributions). The paper also shows that reward institutions can increase both cooperation and efficiency, in contrast to punishment.

- source: Casari, M., & Plott, C. R. (2003). Decentralized management of common property resources: experiments with a centuries-old institution. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a common-pool resource environment with 8 players and continuous contribution/appropriation choices, the addition of a decentralized punishment mechanism (where agents can pay to inspect and fine others for overuse, with fines paid to the inspector) leads to large increases in group efficiency. Without sanctions, efficiency is low (28.4%), below the Nash equilibrium prediction. With weak sanctions, efficiency rises to 57.2% gross (48.3% net after inspection costs). With strong sanctions, efficiency reaches 94% gross (76.9% net). The classical model cannot explain the effect of weak sanctions, but a model with heterogeneous, linear other-regarding preferences (spiteful and altruistic agents) fits the data well. The mechanism channels spiteful behavior into socially useful monitoring. The effect is robust across subject experience and is not due to repeated game effects or communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling decentralized punishment in a common-pool resource game (close variant of a public goods game) can substantially increase efficiency, even when the punishment is relatively weak and should not be effective under standard selfish Nash equilibrium predictions. The effect is robust to subject experience and is not driven by communication or repeated game effects. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are: player_count=8, num_rounds~27-33, no chat, continuous contribution, no default, non-linear MPCR (parabolic then exponential decay), punishment cost and tech specified, no reward, number of rounds not known in advance, summary info provided, and punishment identity not revealed. The control (no-sanction) efficiency is low (28.4%), and the treatment (punishment enabled) efficiency is much higher (48-76% net, 57-94% gross depending on punishment strength). This paper should be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency gains from punishment in similar decentralized, continuous-action, no-communication environments.

- source: Abbink, K., Ryvkin, D., & Serra, D. (2020). Corrupt police. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three main conditions: no police, honest police (can only fine), and corrupt police (can fine or demand bribes). With no police, crime rates are very high and citizen payoffs are low. Honest police reduce crime and increase citizen payoffs. Corrupt police also reduce crime compared to no police, but citizen payoffs are much lower than with honest police due to high bribe payments to officers, resulting in strong inequality. Bribes are used as a substitute for fines, and are often targeted at law-breakers, but imperfectly so—many law-abiding citizens are also extorted. When the maximum bribe is capped at the fine level, crime rates rise to near the no-police level, showing that the deterrent effect of bribes depends on their size. Reward mechanisms for officers based on low bribery rates (but not low crime rates) moderately reduce both bribery and crime, and increase citizen payoffs, but do not eliminate the efficiency loss from corruption.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling punishment (via police, even if corrupt) increases group efficiency compared to no-punishment baselines, but the efficiency gain is much smaller when punishment is corrupt and redistributive (bribes) rather than legal (fines). The efficiency effect of punishment depends on both the targeting and the cost/impact of punishment: when bribes are large and targeted at law-breakers, crime is deterred, but citizen payoffs are much lower due to wealth transfer to officers. If bribes are capped at the fine level, the deterrent effect is lost. Reward mechanisms for officers based on low bribery rates can partially mitigate the efficiency loss from corruption. For prediction, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, but the structure (honest vs. corrupt, bribe size, targeting) and the presence of anti-corruption incentives are critical moderators.

- source: Bravo, G., & Squazzoni, F. (2013). Exit, Punishment and Rewards in Commons Dilemmas: An Experimental Study. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a modified public goods game with six players per group and ten rounds per treatment, the introduction of punishment (a 5 MU fine for non-cooperation) significantly increased cooperation and efficiency compared to the control (no incentives). However, the highest efficiency (profit as a proportion of the theoretical optimum) was achieved when both voluntary participation (exit option) and positive incentives (5 MU reward for cooperation) were present. Punishment alone (No-Neg) led to 67% of the optimum, while reward plus exit (Ex-Pos) led to 69%. The control (No-Null) and punishment plus exit (Ex-Neg) both yielded 58%. Thus, while punishment increases efficiency over the control, it is less effective than the reward-plus-exit combination. The paper also finds that exit alone has little effect, and that the interaction between exit and incentives is important for maximizing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with six players, ten rounds, no communication, and fixed institutional punishment (5 MU fine per defection), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control (no punishment), but the efficiency gain is smaller than that achieved by combining rewards and voluntary participation. The efficiency effect of punishment is positive but not maximal. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, fixed MPCR, fixed punishment cost/tech, no reward in punishment-only treatments, no identification of punishers, and full information about group outcomes) are all specified, making this paper highly informative for predicting efficiency under similar conditions.

- source: Dekel, S., Fischer, S., & Zultan, R. (2017). Potential Pareto Public Goods. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a linear public goods game with two majority players (who benefit from the public good) and one minority player (who is harmed by the public good), punishment institutions do not increase contributions or efficiency. Punishment is used ineffectively, with minority players sometimes punishing contributors, and majority players reluctant to punish free riders. As a result, efficiency is significantly lower in the punishment treatments compared to the baseline. Communication does not improve outcomes in the punishment condition. In contrast, when a reward institution is available and communication is allowed, majority players compensate the harmed minority, which increases majority contributions and efficiency. The presence of a harmed minority undermines the usual positive effects of punishment and communication on cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public goods games with a harmed minority, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency and may even reduce it, relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of communication. The game design features—three players, 30 rounds, continuous contributions, known group composition, and a high punishment impact (3:1 ratio)—do not rescue the effectiveness of punishment in this context. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar settings (especially with harmed minorities), enabling punishment is likely to have a neutral or negative effect on efficiency, even if control efficiency is moderate. The paper also shows that reward institutions, especially with communication, can increase efficiency by enabling compensation.

- source: Wegmann, J., & Musshoff, O. (2019). Groundwater management institutions in the face of rapid urbanization - Results of a framed field experiment in Bengaluru, India. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both externally imposed punishment and reward rules, implemented via random monitoring and sanctions/rewards for exceeding/following optimal extraction, significantly reduce over-extraction of groundwater in a framed field experiment with 600 real-world users. Social efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) increased from 86% in the control to 97% under punishment and 95% under reward, with welfare gains of 8% and 6% over control, respectively (both statistically significant at 1%). Communication alone had a much smaller effect (90% efficiency, 1% gain, not significant). The effect of punishment and reward was not significantly different between rural and urban areas. User types (myopic, rational, social optimal) persisted across treatments, but all treatments crowded out the most pro-social (social optimal) users to some extent. The main outcome is that externally imposed monitoring and sanctioning (punishment) is highly effective at increasing efficiency in this CPR context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that, in a dynamic CPR game with real-world users, adding an externally imposed punishment mechanism (random monitoring, monetary penalty for over-extraction) increases group efficiency (payoff/welfare) by a substantial and statistically significant margin compared to a control with no punishment. The effect is robust to user type and urbanization. The game is not a standard linear PGG but is a close CPR variant with continuous extraction, group interdependence, and profit-based payoffs. The punishment cost and technology are specified, and the efficiency gains are quantified. This evidence should increase predicted efficiency for similar games when punishment is enabled, especially when the control efficiency is below the social optimum.

- source: Vollan, B. (2008). Socio-ecological explanations for crowding-out effects from economic field experiments in southern Africa. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment involved groups of 5 players managing a common-pool resource over 20 rounds, with a baseline (open access) phase followed by an institution phase (external penalty, external reward, or communication) chosen by group vote. Efficiency (group payoff as a percentage of the social optimum) increased from 79.6% in the baseline to 88.6% on average after introducing institutions. However, the effect of punishment (penalty) was highly contingent: when the penalty rule was imposed with low group support (low self-determination), or in high-trust Namibian communities, efficiency actually decreased (crowding-out effect), with final extraction levels as high or higher than the baseline. In contrast, in low-trust South African communities, punishment increased efficiency. Reward (external enabling intervention) did not produce crowding-out in any context and generally improved or maintained efficiency. Communication alone (no external intervention) produced stable, moderate efficiency. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed and moderated by self-determination and social trust.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is not universally positive. Instead, punishment can reduce efficiency (crowding-out) when imposed without group support or in high-trust, norm-driven communities. In low-trust settings, punishment can increase efficiency. Reward mechanisms do not crowd out cooperation and are generally safe for efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment should not be assumed to always increase efficiency; the effect depends on the degree of self-determination (e.g., whether punishment is voted in or imposed) and the baseline level of trust in the group. Control efficiency (baseline) is a strong predictor, but the effect of enabling punishment depends on these moderators. Game design dimensions such as group voting, trust, and the nature of the intervention (controlling vs. enabling) are critical for accurate prediction.

- source: van Miltenburg, N., Przepiorka, W., & Buskens, V. (2017). Consensual punishment does not promote cooperation in the six-person prisoner's dilemma game with noisy public monitoring. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three punishment institutions (individual decision rule, CDR2, CDR3) in a six-person prisoner's dilemma, with and without 20% noise in public monitoring of contributions. In the absence of noise, all punishment institutions increase cooperation and earnings relative to the baseline, with collective decision rules (especially CDR3) performing best. With noise, however, all punishment institutions result in lower earnings than the baseline (no-punishment) condition. The individual decision rule (IDR) produces slightly higher cooperation than collective rules under noise, but still does not improve earnings. The main reason is that noise leads to both mistaken punishment of cooperators and missed punishment of defectors, so resources are wasted and cooperation is not effectively enforced. Thus, punishment does not promote efficiency in noisy environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a six-person all-or-nothing prisoner's dilemma with 20% noise in public monitoring, enabling punishment (regardless of whether it is individual or requires consensus) reduces group efficiency compared to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust across different punishment decision rules. The findings suggest that, when noise is present, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be lower than the control efficiency. The results are directly relevant for settings with similar group size, binary contribution, and noisy monitoring, and less so for linear PGGs or settings with perfect information.

- source: Lohse, J., & Waichman, I. (2020). The effects of contemporaneous peer punishment on cooperation with the future. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a multigenerational public goods game, voluntary cooperation is difficult to sustain across generations. When peer punishment is available within a generation, the likelihood that the group meets the contribution threshold and sustains future generations more than doubles compared to the baseline. However, the increase in group earnings from more sustained generations is offset by the costs incurred through punishment, resulting in no significant difference in average aggregate earnings (efficiency) between the punishment and no-punishment conditions. The effect of punishment on individual contribution levels is small, but it is sufficient to increase the rate at which groups meet the threshold. Punishment is mainly directed at those who contribute less than their peers, especially when the group fails to reach the threshold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling peer punishment in a threshold-based, multigenerational public goods game increases the probability of group success (i.e., reaching future generations and thus group-level payoff), but the efficiency gains are offset by the costs of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of peer punishment can increase the likelihood of group success, but unless punishment is low-cost or highly effective, the net effect on efficiency may be limited or null. The design dimensions—such as group size, threshold, punishment cost and effectiveness, and the absence of communication—are all well-specified and should be used to inform predictions. The results are most relevant for settings with threshold public goods, no communication, and contemporaneous peer punishment.

- source: Abbink, K., Gangadharan, L., Handfield, T., & Thrasher, J. (2017). Peer punishment promotes enforcement of bad social norms. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a linear public goods game with 4 players and either zero (MPCR=0.25) or negative (MPCR=0.2) social returns to contribution, the availability of peer punishment increases contributions. However, in the negative-return condition, this leads to lower group earnings, i.e., punishment enforces a destructive norm. Survey and coordination game evidence show that subjects perceive non-contribution as socially inappropriate, even when it is efficient. Punishment stabilizes these inefficient norms. Thus, punishment does not always improve efficiency; its effect depends on the underlying norm and the payoff structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends critically on the MPCR and the prevailing social norm. In standard PGGs with MPCR > 1/n, punishment can increase efficiency by promoting cooperation. However, when MPCR < 1/n (as here, with MPCR=0.2 and n=4), punishment increases contributions but reduces efficiency, as contributions are socially harmful. For prediction, this means that the sign of punishment's effect on efficiency is not always positive: it can be negative in games where cooperation is inefficient. The paper also provides clear parameter values for player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, and information structure, making it highly informative for prediction tasks.

- source: De Geest, L. R., & Stranlund, J. K. (2019). Defending public goods and common-pool resources. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 2x2 lab experiment (PG/CPR x Theft/No Theft), groups of four insiders played 15 rounds, with or without two outsiders who could steal surplus. Insiders could use costly sanctions (1:3 cost:impact) against outsiders. The main findings are: (a) In the CPR setting, the threat of theft increased cooperation and surplus creation, leading to higher group profits and surplus net of theft compared to the no-theft control. In the PG setting, the threat of theft did not significantly affect cooperation or surplus creation, and group profits decreased. (b) Sanctions were used more often in the PG setting, but were not effective at deterring outsider theft in either setting. (c) The presence of punishment did not restore efficiency to the fully cooperative benchmark, as insiders could not coordinate sanctions well enough to fully deter outsiders. (d) The introduction of theft increased variation in group outcomes, especially in the PG setting. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed: it does not reliably increase efficiency, and its effectiveness depends on the game structure and the ability of insiders to coordinate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency in public-goods-like environments with an external threat (outsider theft). The design allows for direct comparison of efficiency (group profit, surplus net of theft) with and without punishment enabled, under known game parameters. The results show that punishment does not reliably increase efficiency: in the CPR setting, the threat of theft (with punishment available) increases surplus creation and efficiency, but in the PG setting, efficiency decreases. In both cases, punishment is not effective at fully deterring theft, and group variation increases. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is contingent on the type of social dilemma (PG vs CPR), the presence of an external threat, and the group's ability to coordinate sanctions. Control efficiency (no-theft, no-punishment) is higher than treatment efficiency (theft, punishment) in the PG setting, but lower in the CPR setting. The paper is highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in environments with external threats and endogenous punishment.

- source: Javaid, A., & Falk, T. (2015). Incorporating local institutions in irrigation experiments: evidence from rural communities in Pakistan. *Ecology and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implemented a sequential common-pool resource (CPR) game with five players per group, modeling irrigation dilemmas with asymmetric access. In the baseline (no institutional intervention), groups achieved high efficiency (average group earnings at 74% of the social optimum, group investment at 98% of the optimum). When external punishment (costly, probabilistic fines) was introduced, group earnings and efficiency significantly decreased (regression coefficient for group earnings: -9.03, p<0.1). The punishment mechanism reduced over-extraction by head-users but did not increase tail-user earnings; instead, it led to more water being wasted and lower group welfare. Communication and traditional authority treatments did not significantly affect group earnings or investment. The high baseline efficiency is attributed to strong local norms and the public revelation of individual extraction decisions. The main effect of punishment was to create additional coordination problems, not to improve efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments with high baseline efficiency and strong social information (public feedback on individual actions), introducing an external punishment mechanism can reduce group efficiency and welfare. The punishment treatment in this design (costly, probabilistic, not directly linked to rule violations) led to more resource wastage and lower group earnings. For prediction tasks, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is already high and social information is available, enabling punishment may decrease efficiency. The effect is specific to the punishment design used here (costly, probabilistic, not directly rewarding the punisher). Other institutional interventions (communication, traditional authority) had no significant effect. The findings are most applicable to sequential CPR games with asymmetric access, public feedback, and close-knit communities.

- source: Kanitsar, G. (2021). Self-Governance in Generalized Exchange. A Laboratory Experiment on the Structural Embeddedness of Peer Punishment. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Peer punishment increases cooperation and group earnings (efficiency) in public good structures only when punishment is costless; costly punishment does not improve efficiency. In generalized exchange, peer punishment (both costly and costless) does not increase cooperation or group earnings. The effectiveness of punishment is thus highly contingent on the network structure: dense sanctioning networks (public good) allow punishment to deter defection and improve efficiency, while sparse networks (generalized exchange) do not. The cost-impact ratio of punishment is important, but its effect is moderated by the structural environment. The study provides direct evidence that the efficiency gains from peer punishment in public goods do not transfer to generalized exchange settings.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on the network structure. In standard public good games, costless punishment can substantially increase efficiency, but costly punishment may not. In generalized exchange (circular, sparse punishment network), enabling punishment—even if costless—does not improve efficiency. Thus, when predicting the efficiency effect of punishment, it is crucial to account for the density of the punishment network and whether the game is a standard public good or a generalized exchange. Control efficiency is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency when moving from public good to generalized exchange structures.

- source: OSTROM, E., WALKER, J., & GARDNER, R. (1992). COVENANTS WITH AND WITHOUT A SWORD - SELF-GOVERNANCE IS POSSIBLE. *American Political Science Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a CPR game with 8 players and a quadratic production function, baseline efficiency (net yield) is low, especially in high-endowment settings. Allowing communication (covenants) alone increases efficiency substantially, with repeated communication leading to near-optimal outcomes. Introducing a sanctioning mechanism (the sword) alone increases net yield before costs, but after accounting for the costs of punishment, efficiency gains are minimal or negative. When both communication and sanctioning are allowed, especially when the group can choose the sanctioning mechanism, efficiency is maximized and defection rates are minimized. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus highly context-dependent: punishment alone is not efficient, but when combined with communication and endogenous institution-building, it is highly effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends critically on the presence of communication and the way the punishment institution is implemented. If the game design allows for communication and endogenous choice of punishment, enabling punishment can dramatically increase efficiency. If punishment is imposed without communication, efficiency gains are small or negative after accounting for punishment costs. The control efficiency (without punishment) is a strong predictor, but the treatment effect of punishment is positive only when paired with communication and institutional choice. Game design dimensions such as player count (8), number of rounds (20+), absence of chat in baseline, continuous contribution, and the specific punishment cost/tech parameters are all relevant for prediction.

- source: Kosfeld, M., & Rustagi, D. (2015). Leader Punishment and Cooperation in Groups: Experimental Field Evidence from Commons Management in Ethiopia. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This study uses a field experiment with real-world leaders of Ethiopian forest user groups, combining a third-party punishment public goods game (TPPG) with observational data on group performance in commons management. Leaders are classified by their punishment motives: non-punishers, equality-driven, equality-and-efficiency-driven, and antisocial. The main finding is that groups led by equality-and-efficiency-driven punishers achieve substantially higher efficiency (as measured by young trees per hectare) than those led by non-punishers, while groups led by antisocial punishers perform significantly worse. Equality-only punishers have no significant effect in the first assessment but a positive effect in the second. The effect sizes are large relative to the mean outcome. The results are robust to a wide range of controls and alternative specifications. The study highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not uniform: it depends on the leader's punishment motive and implementation.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect is not universally positive or negative, but depends on the nature of punishment. When punishment is targeted at free riders (motivated by equality and/or efficiency), efficiency increases. When punishment is antisocial or indiscriminate, efficiency decreases. The experimental design (two-player linear PGG with third-party punishment, MPCR=0.75, punishment cost=1, punishment impact=3, no chat, continuous contributions) is close to standard PGGs, but the real-world outcome is a field measure of group welfare. The findings suggest that the presence of punishment can increase or decrease efficiency depending on the implementation and motives, and that heterogeneity in punishment type is a key moderator. For prediction, the effect of enabling punishment should be conditioned on whether punishment is likely to be prosocial (targeting defectors) or antisocial (targeting cooperators or indiscriminately).

- source: Cason, T. N., & Gangadharan, L. (2016). Swords without covenants do not lead to self-governance. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment tested four treatments (baseline, punishment only, communication only, both) in a repeated nonlinear common pool resource game with groups of six. Peer punishment (costly, 3:1 fine-to-fee ratio, up to 5 points per target) did not significantly increase group yield or efficiency relative to baseline. Communication (anonymous chat, twice per session) led to dramatic and statistically significant increases in yield/efficiency, with most groups achieving near the social optimum. When both mechanisms were available, communication made punishment largely redundant and unused. The inefficacy of punishment is attributed to the complexity of the nonlinear environment and insufficient use of strong punishment. The findings challenge the generalizability of positive punishment effects from linear public goods games to more realistic, nonlinear settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in nonlinear common pool resource environments (with concave private benefits and endogenous group costs), enabling peer punishment does not improve efficiency over baseline. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar nonlinear games, one should expect little or no efficiency gain, even with a punishment technology that is effective in linear games. The presence of communication is a much stronger moderator, with large positive effects on efficiency. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the payoff structure (linear vs nonlinear), and that control efficiency alone is not sufficient to predict treatment efficiency without considering the game's nonlinearity and the availability of communication.

- source: Mulder, L. B., van Dijk, E., De Cremer, D., & Wilke, H. A. M. (2006). When sanctions fail to increase cooperation in social dilemmas: Considering the presence of an alternative option to defect. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two lab experiments, the authors find that introducing a sanctioning system (punishment for defection) increases collective outcomes (efficiency) in a standard two-option social dilemma (cooperate or defect). However, when a third option to defect (alternative defection, more harmful to the group) is available, sanctions do not increase efficiency and can even decrease it, particularly when participants have low expectations of others' cooperation. The negative effect is mediated by the failure of sanctions to increase trust or expectations of cooperation in the presence of the third option. Thus, the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency is contingent on the decision structure and social expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games is not uniform: it is positive in standard two-choice settings but can be null or negative when alternative, more harmful defection options are available and when group members have low expectations of cooperation. For prediction, this means that the presence of alternative defection options (i.e., more than two choices, with at least one being more harmful to the group) and low trust/expectations are critical moderators. If the control game (no punishment) has low efficiency and the game allows for alternative defection, enabling punishment may not improve and could even worsen efficiency, especially if group trust is low. In standard two-choice games, punishment is likely to increase efficiency.

- source: Aakre, S., Helland, L., & Hovi, J. (2016). When Does Informal Enforcement Work?. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that enabling punishment of both nonparticipants and noncompliant participants (i.e., enforcing both participation and compliance) leads to a substantial increase in total group contributions (efficiency) when punishment is introduced. In contrast, if only one type of free riding can be punished (either only insiders or only outsiders), introducing punishment does not increase efficiency. In the forced participation (FP) control, punishment increases efficiency as in standard PGGs. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is conditional: it is positive only when both participation and compliance can be enforced, and negligible or even negative otherwise.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on whether the game design allows for both participation and compliance enforcement. If the design allows punishment of both nonparticipants and noncompliant participants (or, in standard PGGs, if participation is forced), enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency. If only one type of free riding can be punished, enabling punishment will not increase efficiency. For prediction, the presence or absence of both enforcement channels is a critical moderator. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not sufficient to predict treatment efficiency unless the enforcement regime is specified.

- source: Eisenkopf, G., & Bächtiger, A. (2013). Mediation and Conflict Prevention. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a three-round common-pool resource game with two players, the addition of a third-party mediator who can both communicate and credibly threaten punishment leads to a substantial increase in group efficiency (combined payoff), with most groups achieving the efficient outcome. Communication alone (either direct or via mediator) increases efficiency relative to no mediation, but can also result in greater inequality if one player exploits the other's cooperation. Punishment alone, when costly and not paired with communication, does not improve efficiency. The effectiveness of the punishment-plus-communication treatment is robust to changes in the mediator's interests (impartial, biased, or no-stakes). The study provides direct evidence that communication and punishment are complementary tools for conflict prevention and efficiency in common-pool settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated common-pool resource game with two players, enabling both communication (even indirect, via a mediator) and credible punishment substantially increases efficiency compared to control conditions (no mediation or only one tool enabled). The effect is robust to mediator bias and to the mediator's economic stake. For prediction tasks, the presence of both punishment and communication should be expected to yield much higher efficiency than control, while punishment alone (especially if costly) may not improve efficiency. The game is not a standard linear PGG but is a close variant with continuous extraction and a well-defined efficient outcome, making the findings highly relevant for similar environments.

- source: Janssen, M. A., Holahan, R., Lee, A., & Ostrom, E. (2010). Lab Experiments for the Study of Social-Ecological Systems. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic, spatially explicit CPR lab experiment with five players per group, costly punishment is used by participants but does not increase group payoffs or efficiency unless combined with communication. When punishment is available without communication, net group earnings decrease due to the cost of punishment, and resource depletion is not prevented. Communication alone leads to higher group payoffs and more sustainable resource use. When both communication and punishment are available, the positive effects of communication are not enhanced, and the use of punishment can undermine cooperative agreements once communication is removed. The study highlights that in this more complex, dynamic environment, punishment without communication is not an effective tool for increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in dynamic, spatially explicit CPR games with real-time harvesting and five players, enabling costly punishment without communication is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no punishment, no communication) due to the direct costs of punishment. Communication is a much more effective intervention for increasing efficiency. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled (but no communication) should be lower than the control, and that the presence or absence of communication is a critical moderator. The punishment mechanism here (cost of 1 token to remove 2 tokens from another) is not sufficient to offset the cost in terms of group payoff. These results are most applicable to dynamic, spatially explicit CPR games rather than standard static PGGs, but the payoff-based outcome is directly relevant.

- source: Vollan, B., Pröpper, M., Landmann, A., & Balafoutas, L. (2019). Self-Governance and Punishment: An Experimental Study among Namibian Forest Users. *Economic Development and Cultural Change*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implemented a framed field/lab-in-the-field experiment with Namibian forest users, using a threshold public goods/extraction game with two punishment treatments: internal (peer) and external (random monitoring). Both punishment regimes reduced extraction compared to the baseline, but neither increased group or individual earnings (efficiency). Internal punishment was more effective at disciplining high extractors, especially those of low status, due to more targeted and severe peer punishment. However, internal punishment also led to high levels of antisocial punishment, which, together with the direct costs of punishment, resulted in lower overall earnings than both the external punishment and the no-punishment baseline. Ethnic heterogeneity increased antisocial punishment but did not reduce cooperation or prosocial punishment. The study concludes that while self-governance with peer punishment can discipline free riders, it may also generate substantial welfare losses due to costly and vengeful punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a threshold public goods/extraction game with both internal (peer) and external (centralized) punishment, the introduction of punishment mechanisms does not necessarily increase efficiency (group or individual earnings) and may even reduce it, especially when peer punishment is available. The main reason is that the costs of punishment (both direct and indirect, including antisocial punishment) can outweigh the benefits from increased cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar environments (5 players, 20 rounds, MPCR 0.4, threshold/tipping point, no chat, anonymous punishment, no rewards), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when peer punishment is allowed. The effect may be less negative or neutral with centralized punishment, but still not positive. The findings also highlight the importance of group composition (status, ethnicity) and the potential for antisocial punishment to undermine efficiency gains.

- source: Shreedhar, G., Tavoni, A., & Marchiori, C. (2020). Monitoring and punishment networks in an experimental common pool resource dilemma. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
