  findings: In a nonlinear CPR lab experiment with three punishment network structures (complete, undirected circle, directed circle), the authors find that average appropriation (resource extraction) is similar across all networks. However, the amount and type of punishment, as well as efficiency (net payoffs), differ. The complete network, with perfect monitoring and punishment, leads to the highest punishment expenditures and the lowest efficiency. Both imperfect networks (undirected and directed circles) have lower punishment expenditures and higher efficiency. The undirected circle network elicits more severe and more prosocial punishment than the other two, but overall, both imperfect networks are more efficient than the complete network. The results suggest that reducing the number of potential punishers (i.e., making monitoring and punishment less than perfect) can increase efficiency by reducing costly punishment, even if cooperation (appropriation) does not change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in nonlinear CPR games with punishment enabled, the structure of the punishment network (i.e., the number of potential punishers and the network's density/directedness) can significantly affect efficiency. Specifically, reducing punishment opportunities (imperfect networks) increases efficiency compared to perfect monitoring and punishment, because less punishment is used and thus less is wasted on costly sanctions. Appropriation (cooperation) rates remain similar, so the efficiency gain is due to lower punishment costs. For prediction, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a perfect monitoring/punishment environment may reduce efficiency relative to imperfect punishment environments, and that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not always positive—it depends on the punishment network's structure. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially the structure of punishment opportunities.

- source: Casari, M., & Tavoni, A. (2024). Climate clubs in the laboratory. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares several treatments, including a baseline public goods game (PGG), a version with punishment (PGGwP), and a 'climate club' treatment that combines conditional cooperation and mandatory punishment. The key finding is that adding punishment opportunities (and counter-punishment) does not significantly increase cooperation rates and leads to a statistically significant decrease in net earnings (efficiency) compared to the baseline. The climate club treatment increases cooperation rates but still results in lower net earnings than the baseline, due to the costs associated with punishment. The results are robust across different group compositions and treatments. The paper provides direct evidence that, in this design, punishment reduces efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a three-player, 30-round, binary-contribution public goods game with costly punishment and counter-punishment, enabling punishment reduces group efficiency (net earnings) compared to the control without punishment. The effect is robust and statistically significant. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, under similar design parameters (binary choice, no chat, known rounds, punishment cost per unit = 1, punishment effectiveness varies by target, no rewards), enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency relative to the control. The paper also shows that the presence of counter-punishment does not mitigate this effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the impact of punishment on efficiency in similar lab PGG environments.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Gangadharan, L. (2013). Empowering neighbors versus imposing regulations: An experimental analysis of pollution reduction schemes. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a non-point source pollution problem with heterogeneous agents, four treatments were compared: baseline (no intervention), group tax only, punishment only, and group tax plus punishment. The group tax mechanism (formal regulation) led to large and statistically significant increases in efficiency (from ~55% in baseline to ~87% with tax, and ~90% with tax plus punishment). Peer punishment alone increased efficiency only slightly and not significantly. When combined, punishment reduced variance and helped keep pollution below the target more often, but the efficiency gain was offset by the deadweight loss from punishment costs. Punishment was used less frequently and less intensely than in standard public goods games, limiting its effectiveness. The results suggest that formal regulatory mechanisms are more effective than informal peer punishment in increasing efficiency, but peer punishment can play a complementary role.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with a negative externality (pollution), enabling peer punishment alone yields only modest and inconsistent efficiency gains, while a formal group penalty (tax) mechanism yields large and robust efficiency improvements. Adding punishment to the group tax further reduces damages and increases the likelihood of meeting the target, but does not significantly increase efficiency due to the costliness of punishment. For prediction, the presence of a formal group penalty is the key driver of efficiency, and the marginal effect of enabling peer punishment is small or negligible unless punishment is much more frequent or severe than observed here. The game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment tech, group size, rounds, information) are well-documented, making this study highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in similar settings.

- source: De Geest, L. R., Stranlund, J. K., & Spraggon, J. M. (2017). Deterring poaching of a common pool resource. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing insiders to observe and punish outsiders (poachers) has a non-monotonic effect on efficiency. With zero monitoring (no punishment possible), outsiders show some restraint and insiders achieve moderate efficiency. With partial monitoring (randomly able to punish one outsider), poaching increases over time, sanctions are low and random, and efficiency for insiders drops to Nash equilibrium levels or below. With full monitoring (all outsiders observable and punishable), poaching is lower and more stable, insiders coordinate better, and efficiency is highest, though still far from the social optimum. Sanctions are generally insufficient to fully deter poaching. The results suggest that imperfect punishment opportunities can backfire, reducing efficiency, while full monitoring and punishment can improve but not maximize efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment in common-pool-resource-like games depends critically on the monitoring structure. If punishment is only partially possible (imperfect monitoring), efficiency can decrease relative to no punishment at all, due to increased poaching and poor coordination of sanctions. If punishment is fully enabled (full monitoring), efficiency increases relative to both the control and partial monitoring, but does not reach the social optimum. For prediction, the presence and effectiveness of punishment, the monitoring structure, and the ability to coordinate sanctions are crucial moderators. Control efficiency (no punishment) is not always a lower bound: enabling punishment can reduce efficiency if monitoring is imperfect. Game design dimensions such as punishment cost, punishment tech, group size, communication, and information structure (who sees what) are all relevant. The results caution against assuming that adding punishment always increases efficiency.

- source: Ntuli, H., Crépin, A. S., Schill, C., & Muchapondwa, E. (2023). Sanctioned Quotas Versus Information Provisioning for Community Wildlife Conservation in Zimbabwe: A Framed Field Experiment Approach. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that introducing a sanctioned quota system (with costly punishment for exceeding quotas) leads to significantly higher efficiency in resource use compared to a baseline with no intervention. Information about ecological thresholds also improves efficiency, but not as much as punishment. Combining both interventions does not yield efficiency gains beyond punishment alone. The results are robust across multiple measures (efficiency, group payoff, resource stock, depletion cases) and are supported by regression analysis controlling for individual and group characteristics. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in CPR games with similar design features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that enabling punishment (in the form of sanctioned quotas with costly, probabilistic enforcement) increases efficiency in a dynamic, multi-round, continuous-harvest CPR game with communication allowed. The effect is substantial: average efficiency rises from 0.42 (baseline) to 0.71 (punishment) and 0.77 (punishment+information). The design closely matches public-goods-game-like environments, though the context is a renewable resource with dynamic feedbacks. The results suggest that, for similar games with 4 players, communication, and continuous contribution/harvest, enabling punishment is likely to yield a large positive effect on efficiency, even when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. The effect is robust to the presence of communication and does not depend on end-game knowledge. The paper also provides evidence that the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) and punishment cost/tech are important, but the main result is that punishment is highly effective in this context.

- source: Ostrom, E. (2006). The value-added of laboratory experiments for the study of institutions and common-pool resources. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper reviews a series of laboratory CPR experiments, including baseline (no punishment), communication, exogenous and endogenous sanctioning, and exogenous rule imposition. In baseline (no punishment) games, groups achieve low efficiency (e.g., 37% or less of optimal). When costly punishment is enabled, groups increase their efficiency (e.g., up to 39% of optimal in one set, and up to 94% in another with strong sanctions), but the net gain is reduced by the cost of punishment. Communication and endogenous rule-making (where groups set their own rules and sanctions) lead to the highest efficiency (up to 93% of optimal). Exogenous rules with imperfect monitoring can crowd out cooperation and reduce efficiency. The effect of punishment is sensitive to its cost and effectiveness, and is most positive when combined with communication or endogenous agreement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in CPR-like games increases efficiency relative to control (no punishment), but the magnitude depends on the cost and effectiveness of punishment. If punishment is costly and not highly effective, net efficiency gains may be modest or even offset by sanctioning costs. If punishment is strong (high impact per cost) or is combined with communication or endogenous rule-making, efficiency can approach optimal levels. For prediction, the presence and parameters of punishment (cost, tech), communication, and endogenous rule-making are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is typically low, and the introduction of punishment generally increases efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and/or combined with communication.

- source: De Geest, L. R., & Kingsley, D. C. (2021). Inequality and Peer Punishment in a Common-Pool Resource Experiment. *Strategic Behavior and the Environment*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Peer punishment in a nonlinear CPR game reduces extractions in both equal and unequal endowment settings, but its effect on efficiency (group earnings relative to the social optimum) depends on the distribution of endowments. In the equal endowment treatment, punishment leads to lower earnings due to high punishment costs and lack of coordination. In the unequal endowment treatment, punishment does not reduce average earnings and increases earnings for low-endowment participants, as groups coordinate around the Equal Earnings norm. Punishment is more targeted and less frequent in the unequal treatment, leading to higher efficiency. The effect of punishment is thus moderated by the presence of endowment inequality, which creates a focal point for coordination and more effective norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in CPR/public goods-like games depends on the distribution of endowments (inequality). When endowments are equal, punishment can reduce efficiency due to high punishment costs and poor coordination. When endowments are unequal, punishment is more effective, leading to higher efficiency, especially for low-endowment participants, as groups coordinate around a salient norm. For prediction, the presence of endowment heterogeneity should be considered a key moderator: punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when inequality is present and salient. Other design features (e.g., punishment technology, group size, number of rounds) are held constant in this study, so the main actionable insight is about the interaction between punishment and endowment inequality.

- source: DeCaro, D. A., DeCaro, M. S., Janssen, M. A., Lee, A., Graci, A., & Flener, D. (2024). Learning from regulatory failure: How Ostrom's restorative justice design principle helps naïve groups create wiser enforcement systems to overcome the tragedy of the commons. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Facilitated groups (those guided to discuss enforcement using restorative justice principles) achieved higher group efficiency (net tokens harvested) than unfacilitated groups. The highest efficiency was observed in groups that first experienced regulatory abuse (punishment without communication) and then received facilitation (PFN treatment). Groups that experienced regulatory failure but did not receive facilitation often failed to develop effective enforcement systems and had lower efficiency. The presence of coordinated punishment systems (as opposed to independent or no punishment) was associated with the highest group efficiency. The study demonstrates that both the structure of punishment opportunities and the process of group learning and facilitation are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency in public-goods-like games depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the context in which it is introduced (e.g., prior experience with regulatory failure/abuse) and whether groups receive facilitation to discuss enforcement principles. In prediction tasks, the presence of facilitated discussion (restorative justice), the opportunity for communication, and the structure of the punishment system (cost, effectiveness, coordination) should be considered as moderators. The control efficiency (without punishment) is lower than the treatment efficiency (with punishment and facilitation), especially when groups experience regulatory abuse and are then guided to reflect and coordinate. The findings suggest that punishment can increase efficiency, but only under certain institutional and learning conditions.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., Otaka, T., Shimao, H., Shimomura, K. I., & Yamato, T. (2018). Reciprocity and exclusion in informal financial institutions: An experimental study of rotating savings and credit associations. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared four treatments in a fixed-order ROSCA game: baseline (no sanctions), punishment rule (forfeiture), voting (exclusion), and both punishment and voting. Exclusion by voting led to significantly higher contribution rates and mean payoffs than the other treatments. The punishment rule, which prevented non-contributors from receiving payouts, did not increase contributions or payoffs relative to the baseline. The combination of voting and punishment was less effective than voting alone. The results suggest that social exclusion based on reputation is a more effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation and improving group efficiency than the explicit punishment rule tested here.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong evidence that a costless, rule-based punishment (forfeiture of payout for non-contributors) does not improve efficiency or mean payoff compared to a baseline with no punishment. In contrast, exclusion by peer voting (reputation-based exclusion) substantially increases both contributions and efficiency. The game structure is a close variant of a public goods game (ROSCA), with fixed group size, known rounds, and full information about others' actions. The findings suggest that not all punishment mechanisms are equally effective: exclusion based on group voting is effective, but a non-costly, rule-based punishment is not. This distinction is important for predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Webb, J., & Foddy, M. (2004). Vested interests in the decision to resolve social dilemma conflicts. *Small Group Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The punishment rule (targeted sanctioning) leads to greater resource preservation (more trees left at the end of the game) compared to the equality rule (shared sanctioning), but the equality rule results in higher total group profit. Individual profit outcomes depend on access: high-access participants profit more under the equality rule, while low-access participants profit more under the punishment rule. Voting for structural change (switching sanctioning systems) is predicted by individual profit and fines, not by group-level resource preservation. The study highlights that the impact of sanctioning systems on efficiency and payoff is heterogeneous and depends on both the structure of the sanction and the distribution of resource access.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency and payoff in public-goods-like resource dilemmas depends on the design of the punishment system and the distribution of resource access. If efficiency is defined as resource preservation, targeted punishment (punishment rule) is more effective. If efficiency is defined as total group profit, shared punishment (equality rule) can yield higher payoffs. The results suggest that the effect of enabling punishment is not universally positive or negative for efficiency; it depends on the specific sanctioning mechanism and group heterogeneity. For prediction, the design of the punishment system (targeted vs. shared), the distribution of access, and the definition of efficiency are critical moderators.

- source: van Klingeren, F., & Buskens, V. (2024). Graduated sanctioning, endogenous institutions and sustainable cooperation in common-pool resources: An experimental test. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a dynamic common-pool resource (CPR) game with four players and repeated rounds, the introduction of sanctioning (punishment) mechanisms—either strict or graduated—leads to higher cooperation and resource sustainability compared to no sanctioning. Strict sanctioning (fixed, high-cost punishment) initially results in higher resource size and profit, but graduated sanctioning (punishment severity increases with repeated defection) leads to greater cooperation and lower appropriation over time, eventually closing the gap in resource size and profit. When groups can endogenously choose their sanctioning mechanism (via majority vote every five rounds), cooperation and resource outcomes improve further, especially in the long run. However, the differences in group and individual profit between sanctioning types are small and diminish over time. The study provides partial support for the effectiveness of graduated and endogenously chosen sanctioning, especially for long-term cooperation, but strict sanctioning may be more effective in short games or early rounds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated, dynamic CPR games with endogenous resource dynamics and mutual monitoring. The results suggest that both strict and graduated punishment mechanisms increase efficiency (resource sustainability and group profit) relative to no punishment. Strict punishment is more effective in the short term, but graduated punishment becomes equally or more effective over longer time horizons. Endogenously chosen punishment mechanisms (via group voting) further enhance cooperation and resource outcomes, though the effect on profit is small. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency, with the magnitude and time course depending on the punishment regime and whether the institution is endogenously chosen. The findings are most applicable to settings with 4 players, 40 rounds, continuous contribution/appropriation, no chat, and mutual monitoring.

- source: Milinski, M., & Marotzke, J. (2022). Economic experiments support Ostrom's polycentric approach to mitigating climate change. *Humanities & Social Sciences Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a lab experiment with groups of 9 players, subdivided into 3 subgroups of 3, the opportunity for subgroups to impose costly sanctions on other subgroups (punishment treatment) did not increase the probability of reaching the collective target or improve group payoffs compared to a control without sanctions. Sanctioning was used frequently, but its costs reduced overall payoffs, and it failed to discipline free riders effectively because fair contributors who were also sanctioned reduced their own contributions. Thus, punishment in this polycentric, subgroup-based setting did not improve efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-goods-like collective-risk game with subgroup-level costly punishment, enabling punishment does not increase group efficiency or the probability of reaching the collective target, and may even reduce efficiency due to the costs of punishment and negative effects on fair contributors. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar designs (polycentric, subgroup-based, punishment between subgroups, costly punishment), enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency over the control condition without punishment, and may even be detrimental.

- source: Ozono, H., Jin, N., Watabe, M., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Solving the second-order free rider problem in a public goods game: An experiment using a leader support system. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when leaders are given the ability to punish followers using resources provided by follower support (support-present condition), both the frequency and effectiveness of punishment increase compared to a no-support condition. This leads to a higher probability of groups achieving high cooperation (as measured by contribution rates) and higher average profits for both leaders and followers, especially under 'linkage' punishment leaders who punish both non-cooperators and non-supporters. The no-support condition, where leaders have externally provided punishment resources, results in weaker punishment and lower group cooperation. The findings suggest that the structure of punishment funding and the type of leader punishment strategy are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling leaders to punish using resources provided by group members (rather than external resources) increases the efficiency of punishment in public goods games, as measured by group and individual profits and the frequency of high-cooperation groups. The effect is especially pronounced when leaders punish both first- and second-order free riders. For prediction tasks, the presence of a leader support system, the type of punishment strategy, and the source of punishment capital are key moderators. The control (no-support) condition provides a baseline for efficiency, and the support-present condition demonstrates a substantial positive shift in efficiency when punishment is enabled and properly incentivized.

- source: Gächter, S., Mengel, F., Tsakas, E., & Vostroknutov, A. (2017). Growth and inequality in public good provision. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a dynamic public good game with endogenous endowments, contributions and group wealth increase over time even without punishment. Introducing punishment does not increase group wealth or efficiency; in some cases, it reduces them, particularly in longer games. Punishment increases contributions but destroys resources, leading to lower or unchanged group wealth. Early inequality is strongly negatively correlated with later group wealth in the punishment condition, and anti-social punishment (shirkers punishing contributors) is more prevalent in unequal groups, further reducing efficiency. Removing inequality experimentally restores the positive effect of punishment on contributions and wealth.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in dynamic public good games with endogenous endowments, enabling punishment does not improve and may reduce efficiency compared to control (no punishment). The negative effect is especially likely when early inequality is high, as anti-social punishment becomes more common and destructive. For prediction tasks, if the game design includes endogenous endowments and dynamic wealth accumulation, the presence of punishment should not be expected to increase efficiency, and may decrease it, especially as the number of rounds increases. The effect of punishment is context-dependent: in standard (static, exogenous endowment) PGGs, punishment increases efficiency, but in dynamic, endogenous settings, it does not.

- source: Robbett, A. (2019). Just ask? Preference revelation and lying in a public goods experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares four conditions in a repeated public goods game with private, randomly assigned types: (1) baseline voluntary contribution (VCM), (2) Mechanism (report type to a mechanism that enforces the socially optimal contribution for the reported type), (3) Revelation (report type to group members, then contribute voluntarily), and (4) Punishment (as Revelation, but with a costly peer punishment stage). The main findings are: (a) Mechanism and Revelation both increase cooperation and group efficiency relative to VCM, but Mechanism's effect fades over time; (b) Punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency compared to VCM, and group profits are actually lower in the Punishment condition; (c) Punishment is mainly used to sanction obviously dishonest reports, not under-contribution, and is too weak to incentivize higher contributions; (d) The presence of punishment undermines the positive effect of truthful reporting on cooperation seen in the Revelation condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with private, heterogeneous incentives and observable reports/contributions, adding a peer punishment mechanism (with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio) does not increase efficiency or group payoffs compared to a baseline without punishment. In fact, efficiency is lower with punishment, likely due to underutilization and mis-targeting of sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar environments (3 players, 10 rounds, no chat, private types, punishment available after observing reports/contributions, punishment cost 1:3), enabling punishment is likely to have a neutral or negative effect on efficiency relative to control. The results also highlight that the effectiveness of punishment depends on the clarity of norms and the ability to identify free-riders; when these are ambiguous due to private information, punishment may backfire or be ineffective.

- source: Robbett, A. (2016). Sustaining cooperation in heterogeneous groups. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with heterogeneous, private or observable incentives, peer punishment (sanctions) does not significantly increase group contributions or efficiency above the baseline. This holds both when group members' types are private and when they are observable. In the private information setting, punishment is rarely used except against unambiguous free-riders, and the magnitude is too low to change incentives. When types are observable, punishment is more accurately targeted but still fails to reliably increase contributions, as some high-demanders resist increasing their contributions even when punished. In contrast, both unrestricted communication and a centralized bonus/fine mechanism (Falkinger) substantially increase efficiency, with communication allowing groups to coordinate on the socially optimal contribution vector. The results suggest that punishment is a weak tool for promoting efficiency in heterogeneous groups, especially when contribution norms are ambiguous.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with heterogeneous and possibly private incentives, enabling peer punishment does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff above the baseline. The effect of punishment is weak or null, even when punishment is relatively effective (3:1 impact/cost ratio) and types are observable. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar heterogeneous PGG-like environments, one should expect little or no increase in efficiency relative to the control. The results also highlight the importance of communication and centralized mechanisms as more effective alternatives. The findings are directly relevant for settings with 3 players, 10 rounds, no chat (in punishment treatments), continuous contributions, and a non-linear payoff structure with interior optima.

- source: Cardenas, J. C. (2004). Norms from outside and from inside: an experimental analysis on the governance of local ecosystems. *Forest Policy and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing external regulation (punishment for over-extraction, with imperfect monitoring) or allowing face-to-face communication both lead to substantial increases in efficiency (lower extraction, higher group earnings) compared to a baseline with no intervention. The difference between high and low penalty treatments was much smaller than predicted by standard theory, with both leading to similar improvements in efficiency. A large fraction of participants complied with the social optimum extraction even when the expected cost of punishment was low, indicating that norm compliance and the salience of the rule were important. Communication (self-governance) was as effective as external punishment in increasing efficiency. The results suggest that both material and non-material incentives (norms, communication) can be effective in improving group outcomes in commons dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both punishment (external regulation with fines and monitoring) and communication (self-governance) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, even when the expected cost of punishment is low. The effect of increasing the penalty size (punishment_tech) was much smaller than predicted, suggesting diminishing returns to harsher punishment. The game design dimensions (player_count=5, num_rounds=20, no chat in baseline, chat in self-governance, continuous extraction, known payoff table, monitoring probability, and fine size) are all well specified, making this paper highly informative for predicting efficiency under different punishment regimes. Control efficiency (no punishment, no communication) is lower than in the treatments, and both punishment and communication increase efficiency to similar levels.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2013). If Cooperation Is Likely Punish Mildly: Insights from Economic Experiments Based on the Snowdrift Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This study uses repeated group snowdrift games (a close variant of public goods games) with and without punishment to test the effects of mild versus severe punishment on cooperation and average payoff. In groups of five, with 25 rounds per session, the authors manipulate the cost-to-benefit ratio (r = 0.2 or 0.8) and the punishment fine (p = 2.0 or 4.0, with constant cost to punisher). When cooperation is likely (r = 0.2), both mild and severe punishment increase cooperation, but severe punishment does not further increase cooperation and reduces average payoff compared to mild punishment. When cooperation is unlikely (r = 0.8), only severe punishment substantially increases cooperation and average payoff; mild punishment is less effective and can even reduce average payoff compared to no punishment. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: mild punishment is optimal when cooperation is already likely, while severe punishment is only justified in highly defection-prone environments.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the underlying difficulty of cooperation (cost-to-benefit ratio, analogous to MPCR in PGGs). If the control game (no punishment) already achieves high efficiency, adding severe punishment will not improve and may reduce efficiency; mild punishment is sufficient. If the control game has low efficiency (cooperation is rare), only severe punishment can increase efficiency. The cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the baseline cooperation rate, are critical moderators. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency in snowdrift- or PGG-like games with peer punishment.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2019). Individual solutions to shared problems create a modern tragedy of the commons. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can choose between solving a shared problem collectively (public good) or individually (private solution), many opt for the individual solution even when collective action would be more efficient. This leads to inefficient resource allocation and coordination failure, especially at intermediate cost-benefit ratios. When peer punishment is introduced, it increases the rate of collective solutions but also triggers mutual punishment between individualists and collectivists, which reduces overall group welfare. Punishment does not reduce free riding but instead is used in feuds between incompatible strategies, amplifying inefficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public-goods-like games where individual solutions are available, enabling peer punishment can reduce efficiency (group welfare), even if it increases collective action. The effect is driven by wasteful punishment exchanges rather than improved cooperation. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency in similar settings, especially when individual solutions are a viable alternative to collective action. The findings are most relevant when the game allows both individual and collective solutions, and punishment is peer-based, costly, and has a strong impact.

- source: Kamei, K., Putterman, L., & Tyran, J. R. (2023). Civic engagement, the leverage effect and the accountable state. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a large-group lab experiment, participants first face a public goods allocation problem (main stage) and can only enable a central punishment institution (which enforces optimal contributions) if enough group members voluntarily complete costly 'civic tasks' in a pre-stage (second-order dilemma). When the punishment institution is absent, group efficiency is low (public sector allocations rapidly decline to ~13% of the optimum). When the institution is present, allocations and group earnings approach the social optimum. The probability of achieving the institution is much higher when the cost of civic engagement is low and when social interaction is allowed. The study demonstrates that the presence of a punishment institution, conditional on sufficient civic engagement, robustly increases efficiency in public goods provision. The effect is strongest when the cost of civic engagement is low and when social feedback is available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a central punishment institution in a public-goods-like environment dramatically increases efficiency, moving group payoffs close to the social optimum. The effect is conditional on the institution being present, which depends on a prior (second-order) cooperation problem. The efficiency gain from punishment is robust across treatments, but the likelihood of achieving the institution (and thus the efficiency gain) is higher when the cost of enabling punishment is low and when social interaction is present. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, given a design where punishment is enabled (i.e., the institution is present), efficiency will be much higher than in the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is moderated by the cost of enabling punishment and by the presence of social interaction, but the direction is consistently positive for efficiency.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Chen, Z., Perc, M., Wang, Z., Kurths, J., & Moreno, Y. (2023). Deterrence through punishment can resolve collective risk dilemmas in carbon emission games. *Chaos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a collective-risk public goods game (carbon emission game), costly punishment of free-riders (with fines imposed probabilistically and costs shared by all group members) increases the probability that groups reach the collective target, but only when the punishment risk is high (p > 0.8). At low punishment risk, deterrence is weak, and group failure rates remain high. Larger groups are less likely to succeed at any given punishment risk, requiring higher punishment risk for similar deterrence. The introduction of punishment shifts most players to fair-share contributions, reducing both free-riding and maximal altruism. The effect of punishment on group success (a proxy for efficiency) is robust to different loss probabilities. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, but the probability of reaching the target and average investment are closely related and show a positive effect of punishment when sufficiently strong.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in collective-risk public goods games with a threshold target and probabilistic costly punishment, enabling punishment increases group success rates (and thus efficiency) only when the punishment risk is high. The effect is moderated by group size (larger groups require higher punishment risk) and is robust to different loss probabilities. For prediction, the presence and strength of punishment (punishment risk, fine size, cost structure), group size, and the absence of communication are key moderators. The control (no-punishment) condition yields low group success rates, especially as group size increases. The findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency when punishment is strong and credible, but little or no effect when punishment is weak or infrequent.

- source: Nair, A., Blome, C., Choi, T. Y., & Lee, G. (2018). Re-visiting collaborative behavior in supply networks structural embeddedness and the influence of contextual changes and sanctions. *Journal of Purchasing and Supply Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated triadic supply network game with real managers, the introduction of sanctions (punishment) reduces both group allocation and average payoffs when the incentive structure favors cooperation (i.e., low temptation to defect). When the incentive structure is changed to increase the temptation to defect, sanctions increase group allocation compared to the no-sanction condition, but average payoffs remain lower than in the cooperative baseline. Sanctions are only effective at increasing group allocation when the underlying incentive structure is unfavorable to cooperation. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average payoffs are provided for each condition. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus negative in cooperative contexts and ambiguous (possibly less negative) in defect-prone contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like network games depends critically on the underlying incentive structure. When the baseline (control) efficiency is high (cooperative context), enabling punishment reduces efficiency. When the baseline efficiency is low (defection is tempting), enabling punishment can increase group allocation but does not fully restore efficiency/payoff to cooperative levels. For prediction, this suggests that the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency is negative when control efficiency is high, and less negative or possibly weakly positive when control efficiency is low, but the overall efficiency with punishment rarely exceeds the cooperative baseline. The game design dimensions most relevant here are the payoff structure (temptation to defect), the presence and cost/effectiveness of punishment, and the information structure.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a voluntary Mutual Aid game (a variant of the public goods game), groups can choose between no punishment, peer punishment, pool punishment, or non-participation. Pool punishment only becomes dominant and sustains high cooperation if it includes second-order punishment (punishing those who do not contribute to the punishment pool). In this treatment, cooperation rates are very high (88.2%) and average payoffs for pool and peer punishment are similar and high. In the absence of second-order punishment, pool punishment is not adopted, cooperation rates are lower (48.9%), and peer punishment is preferred but less stable. Peer punishment is more efficient when cooperation is achieved, but pool punishment with second-order punishment is more stable and leads to higher sustained cooperation and payoffs. Players adapt their choices based on observed payoffs, indicating social learning. The results highlight the importance of sanctioning second-order free-riders for the success of institutional punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency (group payoff) in public-goods-like games depends critically on the design of the punishment institution, especially whether second-order free-riders are sanctioned. When pool punishment includes second-order punishment, it leads to high and stable cooperation and payoffs, outperforming peer punishment in stability (though not always in efficiency if all cooperate). Peer punishment is initially attractive but declines over time. The findings suggest that, for prediction, the presence of second-order punishment, the type of punishment (peer vs. pool), and the ability to opt out are key moderators. Control efficiency (no punishment) is much lower than with effective (second-order) pool punishment. The results are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency in games with similar design features.

- source: Grimalda, G., Belianin, A., Hennig-Schmidt, H., Requate, T., & Ryzhkova, M. (2022). Sanctions and international interaction improve cooperation to avert climate change. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment tested the effect of sanctions (costly punishment) in a repeated collective-risk social dilemma with German and Russian participants, both in national and international groups. Sanctions increased group cooperation (as measured by the probability of loss avoidance, PLA), especially for Russian participants and in international groups. However, the cost of sanctioning meant that final payoffs were actually higher in the no-sanction treatments, as shown in the supplementary material. The effect of sanctions was positive for increasing cooperation and loss avoidance, but negative for monetary efficiency. The combination of international interaction and sanctions was particularly effective at raising cooperation among low cooperators (Russians), leading to convergence with high cooperators (Germans). The effect of revealing nationality was minimal. Sanctioning was mostly prosocial in international groups, and antisocial sanctioning (punishing high contributors) was reduced. Only a small fraction of the endowment was spent on sanctions, but this was sufficient to increase cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling costly punishment (sanctions) in a repeated collective-risk social dilemma increases group cooperation (PLA) but reduces final payoffs due to the cost of sanctioning. For prediction tasks, this means that the efficiency of the punishment-enabled game may be higher or lower than the control, depending on whether the benefit from increased loss avoidance outweighs the cost of sanctions. In this experiment, the cost of sanctions was high enough that final payoffs were higher in the no-sanction condition, even though cooperation (PLA) was higher with sanctions. The effect is especially pronounced for groups with low baseline cooperation, and in international groups. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence and cost of punishment, group composition (nationality/culture), and the structure of the risk/loss. The game is a close variant of a PGG, but with a threshold and probabilistic loss, so direct transfer to standard PGGs should be cautious.

- source: Khadjavi, M., Lange, A., & Nicklisch, A. (2017). How transparency may corrupt - experimental evidence from asymmetric public goods games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in an asymmetric public goods game (one official can embezzle, others can only contribute), transparency alone (without punishment) increases embezzlement by the official and reduces public good provision. Introducing a peer punishment stage (where group members can spend resources to reduce others' payoffs) increases public good provision and curbs embezzlement, but only if the official's identity is known. When both identity and actions are transparent (high transparency), punishment is more accurately targeted, reducing stigmatization and further increasing cooperation. Mean income is higher in punishment treatments, and income inequality between officials and citizens is reduced when punishment is possible and the official's identity is known.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling peer punishment in a public goods game with asymmetric roles (embezzlement possible for one player) increases group payoff and public good provision, especially when the official's identity is known. The effect is robust across both low and high transparency, but high transparency reduces erroneous punishment (stigmatization) and allows more targeted sanctions. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio), group size of 4, 10 rounds, and no communication, is associated with a substantial increase in efficiency-related outcomes compared to control. The effect is strongest when both identity and actions are transparent, but even low transparency with punishment is effective. Control efficiency (without punishment) is lower under high transparency due to increased embezzlement. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in asymmetric PGG-like environments with similar design features.

- source: Bracht, J., Figuières, C., & Ratto, M. (2008). Relative performance of two simple incentive mechanisms in a public goods experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper experimentally compares two incentive mechanisms for public goods provision: the Falkinger mechanism (which rewards/penalizes deviations from average contributions) and the compensation mechanism (where players can subsidize each other's contributions). Both mechanisms increase contributions and group efficiency compared to the voluntary contribution control. The Falkinger mechanism, particularly with higher tax-subsidy rates (closer to the efficient value), leads to significantly higher and more stable efficiency gains than the compensation mechanism. The compensation mechanism initially boosts contributions, but this effect decays over time, and average efficiency remains lower than under the Falkinger mechanism. The Falkinger mechanism's performance is robust to parameter changes and predicts average contributions well, while the compensation mechanism suffers from suboptimal subsidy rates and coordination issues.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment-and-reward mechanism (Falkinger) in a two-player, repeated, continuous-contribution public goods game with full information and no communication substantially increases efficiency relative to the control. The effect is stronger and more reliable when the punishment/reward parameter is set closer to the efficient value. The compensation (reward-only) mechanism also increases efficiency, but less so, and the effect is less stable. For prediction, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment/reward mechanisms on efficiency, especially when the mechanism is well-calibrated. The results are most directly applicable to small, fixed pairs, continuous contribution, no chat, and full feedback settings.

- source: Gelcich, S., Guzman, R., Rodríguez-Sickert, C., Castilla, J. C., & Cárdenas, J. C. (2013). Exploring External Validity of Common Pool Resource Experiments: Insights from Artisanal Benthic Fisheries in Chile. *Ecology and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: This paper reports a field-lab experiment with Chilean artisanal fishers playing a 20-round common pool resource game. In the first 10 rounds (open access, no enforcement), overharvesting increased and efficiency declined, especially among low-performance unions and nonunionized fishers. In rounds 11-20, a weak enforcement regime was introduced: two players per round were randomly inspected, and if caught overharvesting, their entire round's harvest was confiscated. This sanction led to a sharp reduction in overharvest and a large increase in group earnings (efficiency) among unionized fishers, especially those from high-performance unions, who sustained cooperation and high efficiency through the end of the game. Low-performance unions showed a temporary improvement but cooperation eroded over time. Nonunionized fishers did not respond to enforcement and remained inefficient. The paper provides direct evidence that even weak sanctions can improve efficiency in CPR settings, but the effect is strongly moderated by group social capital and prior cooperative norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment/sanction mechanism (even if weak and probabilistic) in a repeated CPR game with continuous contribution/extraction choices and no communication can substantially increase group efficiency, especially when baseline efficiency is not already at floor. The effect is strongest in groups with high social capital or prior cooperative norms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment in similar CPR/Pgg-like games will increase efficiency, with the magnitude depending on group composition and baseline cooperativeness. The design features—5 players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous extraction, no rewards, and a probabilistic, high-impact punishment—are all relevant for mapping to prediction. The control (no punishment) condition yields low efficiency, while the treatment (punishment enabled) yields much higher efficiency for unionized groups.

- source: Zhosan, D., & Gardner, R. (2013). Problems of the commons: group behavior, cooperation and sanctioning in a two-harbor experiment. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment simulating a two-commons (two-harbor) resource dilemma, groups of 8 participants (students and professional fishermen) repeatedly chose how to allocate effort between two commons and a safe alternative. The experiment varied institutional features across blocks: baseline (no restrictions), enforcement (costly movement between commons), communication, and communication plus sanctioning. Efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) was the primary outcome. Communication led to large, significant increases in efficiency for all groups, with average net efficiency rising from ~0.8 to ~0.98. The addition of a sanctioning mechanism (warnings, then monetary penalties for repeated defection) further increased efficiency in most groups, sometimes reaching 1.0. The effect of sanctioning was generally positive but smaller than the effect of communication, and in one group, sanctioning led to a breakdown in cooperation. The results show that communication and sanctioning can both be effective in increasing efficiency in commons dilemmas, with communication having the largest and most consistent effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication among group members in a commons dilemma can dramatically increase efficiency, even before any formal or monetary sanctioning is introduced. The addition of a sanctioning mechanism (warnings and monetary penalties) yields further, though more modest, efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, the presence of communication and/or sanctioning should be expected to increase efficiency substantially relative to a control with neither. The effect of communication is robust and large; the effect of sanctioning is positive but may be context-dependent and sometimes less pronounced. The experiment's design and reporting allow for direct mapping to game design dimensions relevant for prediction.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2018). Managing and defending the commons: Experimental evidence from TURFs in Chile. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common-pool resource game with insiders (who can communicate) and outsiders (who cannot), the introduction of poaching without monitoring or sanctions dramatically reduces insiders' earnings and resource sustainability, nearly eliminating the efficiency gains from cooperation. Adding weak external enforcement (low-probability monitoring and fines) or allowing insiders to invest in monitoring (with the same fine) both significantly reduce poaching and increase insiders' earnings and harvests, but do not fully restore efficiency to baseline (no-poaching) levels. Combining external and local monitoring does not further reduce poaching or increase efficiency. The main constraint is insiders' inability to coordinate sufficient monitoring investment. The results are robust across field (TURF members) and student samples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a dynamic CPR game with communication among insiders, the introduction of punishment (via monitoring and fines for poaching) increases group efficiency relative to a poaching-only control, but the effect is partial and depends on the ability to coordinate monitoring. The effect is similar whether monitoring is provided externally or by insiders, and combining both does not yield additional efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment (even if weak) should be expected to increase efficiency above the poaching-only baseline, but not to the level of the no-poaching or full-cooperation baseline. The magnitude of the effect is limited by the monitoring probability and the coordination of monitoring investment.

- source: Makowsky, M. D., & Wang, S. Y. (2018). Embezzlement, whistleblowing, and organizational architecture: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment with 6-player groups, participants played a common pool resource game with a whistleblowing option: after each withdrawal, any non-first-mover could choose to 'blow the whistle', setting all group payoffs to zero. The game was played under six different organizational structures, varying the number of sequential levels (from 1 to 6) and the arrangement of players (pyramid, inverted pyramid, flat, etc.). The main findings are: (1) Embezzlement rates are high in all structures, but increase with the number of sequential levels; (2) The probability of group-level punishment (whistleblowing) increases with the number of levels; (3) Average group income (efficiency) decreases as the number of levels increases; (4) Flatter (simultaneous) structures have higher efficiency than taller (sequential) ones; (5) Pyramid structures have lower embezzlement than inverted pyramids, but this does not translate into significant efficiency differences due to similar rates of group-level punishment. The punishment mechanism is highly costly and does not improve efficiency; rather, it reduces it further when triggered.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with a costly, group-level punishment mechanism (whistleblowing that sets all payoffs to zero), enabling punishment does not improve efficiency and may reduce it, especially as the game becomes more sequential (taller). The efficiency loss is driven by both high embezzlement and the destructive nature of the punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, when the control (no-punishment) game already has low efficiency due to high embezzlement, adding a highly destructive punishment option (like whistleblowing) is likely to further reduce efficiency, particularly in games with more sequential moves or more levels. The design dimensions most relevant are the number of players, number of rounds, the structure of play (sequential vs. simultaneous), and the nature/cost of punishment. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is costly and group-level (not targeted), and less so to standard peer-to-peer punishment in classic PGGs.

- source: Freytag, A., Güth, W., Koppel, H., & Wangler, L. (2014). Is regulation by milestones efficiency enhancing? An experimental study of environmental protection. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This laboratory experiment tests whether adding intermediate contribution targets ('milestones') to a cumulative threshold public goods game increases efficiency, measured by group payoffs and the probability of reaching a final target. The game is played in groups of 5 over 6 rounds, with treatments varying the strictness of milestones, the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), and the probability of group loss if targets are missed. The main findings are: (1) In a scenario with no continuous efficiency gains (i.e., only reaching the target matters), strict milestones increase both contributions and the probability of reaching the target, thus enhancing efficiency. (2) In a scenario with continuous efficiency gains (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones actually reduce average contributions and efficiency, as groups focus on meeting milestones rather than maximizing payoffs. (3) In an intermediate scenario, the positive and negative effects cancel out. The effect of milestones is thus context-dependent, and their efficiency impact is not universally positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment-like milestone mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the underlying payoff structure. When the only way to generate group payoffs is to reach a threshold (no continuous efficiency gains), strict milestones (punishment for missing intermediate targets) can improve efficiency by increasing coordination and contributions. However, when continuous efficiency gains are present (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones can reduce efficiency, as they may distract from maximizing group welfare. For prediction, this means that the presence and structure of milestone/punishment mechanisms, the marginal per-capita return, and the risk of group loss must all be considered. Control efficiency (without milestones/punishment) is a strong moderator: if control efficiency is already high, milestones may not help and can even hurt. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions in cumulative threshold public goods games with milestone/punishment features.

- source: Sääksvuori, L. (2014). Intergroup conflict, ostracism, and the evolution of cooperation under free migration. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper experimentally studies a dynamic public goods game with endogenous group formation, free migration, and treatments varying the presence of intergroup competition and ostracism (social exclusion by majority vote). The main findings are: (1) Ostracism increases average group size and contributions to the public good, resulting in higher net payoffs and aggregate returns, both with and without intergroup competition. (2) Intergroup competition alone does not increase group size or contributions, and leads to more imbalanced group structures, which reduces aggregate returns. (3) The combination of ostracism and competition still produces higher cooperation and payoffs than competition alone. (4) The threat of ostracism reduces the number of free-agents and increases the desirability of joining groups, outweighing the effect of involuntary exclusion. (5) Intergroup competition reduces the likelihood of ostracism, as groups are less willing to expel members when facing external threats.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling a social sanctioning mechanism (ostracism) in a dynamic, migration-allowed public goods environment increases both cooperation and group-level payoffs (i.e., efficiency), regardless of whether intergroup competition is present. The effect of ostracism is robust and positive for efficiency-related outcomes. Intergroup competition alone does not improve efficiency and may even reduce it by fragmenting groups. The design features—such as endogenous group formation, free migration, and ostracism by majority vote—are important moderators. The paper does not use classic costly punishment (with explicit cost per punishment point), but ostracism functions as a group-level sanction. Thus, for environments with similar group formation and migration, the presence of ostracism is a strong positive predictor for efficiency gains from sanctioning.
